# Nigeria

This article is about the country. For other uses, see [Nigeria (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria_\(disambiguation\)).

"Naijá" redirects here. For the language sometimes referred to by the same name, see [Nigerian Pidgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Pidgin).

Not to be confused with the neighbouring country [Niger](21373.md).

**Nigeria**,[^cite_note-11] officially the **Federal Republic of Nigeria**, is a country in [West Africa](67393.md) between the [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel) to the north and the [Gulf of Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea) in the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) to the south. It covers an area of 923,769 square kilometres (356,669 mi<sup>2</sup>). With [a population](21386.md) of more than 242 million, it is the [most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population) in [Africa](5334607.md), and the [world's sixth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population). Nigeria borders [Niger](21373.md) in [the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Nigeria_border), [Chad](5488.md) in [the northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad%E2%80%93Nigeria_border), [Cameroon](5447.md) in [the east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon%E2%80%93Nigeria_border), and [Benin](3459.md) in [the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin%E2%80%93Nigeria_border). Nigeria is a [federal republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) comprising 36 [states](226734.md) and the [Federal Capital Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Capital_Territory,_Nigeria), where its capital, [Abuja](56823.md), is located. The [largest city in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nigerian_cities_by_population) by population is [Lagos](85232.md), one of the largest [metropolitan areas](14649921.md) in the world and the [second largest in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Africa_by_population).

Nigeria is a [multinational state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_state) inhabited by more than [250 ethnic groups](21386.md) speaking [500 distinct languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Nigeria), all identifying with a wide variety of cultures.[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14] The three largest ethnic groups are the [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_people), [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people), and [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) together constituting over 60% of the total population.[^cite_note-15] The [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) is [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_English).[^cite_note-16] [Nigeria's constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nigeria) ensures [freedom of religion](46006.md).[^cite_note-17] The country is divided roughly in half between [Muslims](19541.md) and [Christians](18337522.md); [indigenous religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religions), such as those native to the [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odinani) and [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_religion) ethnicities, are in the minority.[^cite_note-USEN-18]

Nigeria has been home to several indigenous material cultures, [pre-colonial states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_traditional_rulers) and [kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahelian_kingdoms) since the second [millennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennium) BC. The [Nok culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nok_culture), c. 1500 BC, marks one of the earliest known civilisations in the region. The [Hausa Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms) inhabited the north, with the [Edo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_people) [Kingdom of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Benin) in the south, the [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) [Kingdom of Nri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nri) in the southeast, and in the southwest the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) [Empire of Oyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire). The present-day territory of Nigeria was home to a vast array of [city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state). In the early 19th century, the [Fulani Jihads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fula_jihads) culminated in the [Sokoto Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate). The modern state originated with [British colonialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Nigeria) in the 19th century, taking its present territorial shape with the merging of the [Southern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Nigeria_Protectorate) and the [Northern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Nigeria_Protectorate) in 1914. The [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) set up administrative and legal structures and [incorporated traditional monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Chieftaincy) as a form of [indirect rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indirect_rule).[^cite_note-19]

Nigeria officially gained [independence](62450.md) as a federation on 1 October 1960. From 1967 to 1970, the country was ravaged by [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War), followed by a series of [military dictatorships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Nigeria) (as well as an unstable [democracy from 1979 to 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehu_Shagari)), until a stable democracy was established in 1999. However, the work of the [Nigerian Electoral Commission (INEC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_National_Electoral_Commission_\(Nigeria\)) has been criticised both nationally and internationally for lacking transparency.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]

Nigeria is one of Africa’s largest economies, alongside [South Africa](17416221.md) and [Egypt](8087628.md). It is regarded as an [emerging market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_market) by the [World Bank](36752.md). Nigeria is a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) in Africa and a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) in international affairs. Nigeria is a founding member of the [African Union](10634933.md) and a member of many international organisations, including the [United Nations](31769.md), the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations), [NAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement),[^cite_note-22] the [Economic Community of West African States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States), [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) and [OPEC](166346.md).

## Etymology

The name *[Nigeria](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Nigeria)* derives from the [Niger River](21437.md) running through the country. This name was coined on 8 January 1897, by the British journalist [Flora Shaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_Shaw,_Lady_Lugard). The neighbouring [Republic of Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Niger) takes its name from the same river. The origin of the name *[Niger](21373.md)*, which originally applied to only the middle reaches of the Niger River, is uncertain. The word is likely an alteration of the [Tuareg](103054.md) name *egerew n-igerewen* ("river of rivers") used by inhabitants along the middle reaches of the river around [Timbuktu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timbuktu) before 19th-century [European colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa).[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24] Before Flora Shaw suggested the name *Nigeria*, other proposed names included *Royal Niger Company Territories*, *Central Sudan*, *Niger Empire*, *Niger Sudan*, and *Hausa Territories*.[^cite_note-25]

## History

Main articles: [History of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nigeria) and [Timeline of Nigerian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Nigerian_history)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistory of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Nigeria)



[Nok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nok) sculpture, [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta)

[Kainji Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kainji_Dam) [excavations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_excavation) showed ironworking by the 2nd century BC. The transition from [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) times to the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) was accomplished without intermediate [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) production. Some have suggested the technology moved west from the [Nile Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Valley). But the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_metallurgy_in_Africa) in the [Niger River](21437.md) valley and the forest region appears to predate the introduction of metallurgy in the upper savanna by more than 800 years, as well as predating it in the Nile Valley. More recent research suggests that iron metallurgy was developed independently in [Africa](5334607.md).[^cite_note-Tylecote_1975_see_below-26][^cite_note-Eggert_2014_51–59-27][^cite_note-Eggert_2014_53–54-28][^cite_note-Holl-2020#2-29]

The [Nok civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nok_culture) thrived between 1500 BC and 200 AD.[^cite_note-30] It produced life-sized [terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta) figures that are some of the earliest known sculptures in Africa[^cite_note-PB_2014-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-archaeology-34][^cite_note-35] and independently invented iron smelting by about 550 BC and possibly a few centuries earlier.[^cite_note-Tylecote_1975_see_below-26][^cite_note-Eggert_2014_51–59-27][^cite_note-Eggert_2014_53–54-28] Evidence of iron smelting has also been excavated at sites in the [Nsukka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nsukka) region of southeast Nigeria: dating to 2000 BC at the site of Lejja[^cite_note-Eze-Uzomaka-36] and to 750 BC at the site of [Opi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opi_\(archaeological_site\)).

### Early history

Main article: [History of Nigeria before 1500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nigeria_before_1500)

The *[Kano Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_Chronicle)* highlights an ancient history dating to around 999 AD of the [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms) city state of [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kano), with other major Hausa cities (or [Hausa Bakwai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Bakwai)) of [Daura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daura), [Biram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadejia), [Katsina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Katsina), [Kingdom of Zazzau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Zazzau), [Rano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rano), and [Gobir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobir) all having recorded histories dating back to the 10th century.

The [Kingdom of Nri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nri) of the Igbo people consolidated in the 10th century and continued until it lost its sovereignty to the British in 1911.[^cite_note-Juang3-37][^cite_note-38] Nri was ruled by the [Eze Nri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rulers_of_Nri), and the city of Nri is considered to be the foundation of [Igbo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_culture). Nri and [Aguleri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aguleri), where the Igbo creation myth originates, are in the territory of the Umeuri clan. Members of the clan trace their lineages back to the patriarchal king-figure [Eri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eri_\(king\)).[^cite_note-39] In West Africa, the oldest bronzes made using the [lost wax process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_wax_process) were from [Igbo-Ukwu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo-Ukwu), a city under Nri influence.[^cite_note-Juang3-37]



Royal [Benin ivory mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_ivory_mask), one of Nigeria's most recognised artifacts. [Benin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_Empire), 16th century.

The [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) had developed one of the earliest [city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state) in Africa by the 8th century known as [Ile Ife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/If%E1%BA%B9), which would become the heart of the later [Ife Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ife_Empire) while [Oyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire) and other Yoruba states became more prominent in the 12th[^cite_note-Falola_Heaton_Early_states_and_societies-40][^cite_note-41] and 14th[^cite_note-42] centuries, respectively. The oldest signs of [human](682482.md) settlement at Ife's current site date back to the 9th century,[^cite_note-Falola_Heaton_Early_states_and_societies-40] and its material culture includes [Terracotta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terracotta) and Bronze figures. The [Kingdom of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Benin), founded by the [Edo people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_people), was a major West African power. Its densely populated capital ([Benin City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_City)) served as a central hub for regional and transatlantic trade in cloth, ivory, pepper, and salt.[^cite_note-43]

Written documentation of Nigerian history from native sources dates back to at least the 14th century. In the 1390s, [Mai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mais_of_Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu) [Biri III Uthman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biri_III_Uthman) wrote a letter to [Barquq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barquq), Sultan of [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate) at the time.[^cite_note-\:17-44] He complained about slave raids on the local Muslim population by [Judham Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Judham), who he described as polytheists who deviated from true religion.[^cite_note-\:17-44]

The [Kanem–Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire) was a long-lived major Islamic power in the region. Originally based in the [Kanem region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem_\(historical_region\)), the ruling dynasty relocated southwest to [Borno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bornu_\(historical_region\)) in the late fourteenth century. Kanem–Bornu established extensive diplomatic and trade networks reaching North Africa and Europe. Under rulers such as Mai [Idris Alooma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idris_Alooma), much of Hausaland was brought into tributary status under Borno.[^cite_note-Siollun_2025-45]

### Pre-colonial era

Further information: [History of Nigeria (1500–1800)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nigeria_\(1500%E2%80%931800\))

In the 16th century, [Portuguese explorers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_discoveries) were the first [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe) to begin important, direct trade with the peoples of southern Nigeria, at the port they named [Lagos](85232.md) (formerly Eko) in [Yoruba land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorubaland) and in [Calabar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calabar) along the region [Slave Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_Coast_of_West_Africa). Europeans traded goods with peoples at the coast; coastal trade with Europeans also marked the beginnings of the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-diverse-slavery-46] The port of Calabar on the historical [Bight of Biafra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bight_of_Biafra) (now commonly referred to as the Bight of Bonny) became one of the largest slave-trading posts in West Africa in this era. Other major slaving ports were located in [Badagry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badagry), Lagos on the [Bight of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bight_of_Benin), and [Bonny Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonny_Island) on the Bight of Biafra.[^cite_note-diverse-slavery-46][^cite_note-slave-trade-47] The majority of those taken to these ports were captured in raids and wars.[^cite_note-48] Usually, the captives were taken back to the conquerors' territory as [forced labour](541048.md); they were sometimes gradually acculturated and absorbed into the conquerors' society. Slave routes were established throughout Nigeria linking the hinterland areas with the major coastal ports. Several kingdoms that directly or indirectly participated in the Atlantic slave trade were the [Benin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Benin) in the south, the [Oyo Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire) in the southwest, and the [Aro Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aro_Confederacy) in the southeast. In contrast, the kingdoms associated with the [Trans-Saharan slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Saharan_slave_trade) were the [Hausa states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms), as well as the [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_people)-led [Kanem-Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem-Bornu_Empire).[^cite_note-Savage1992-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-diverse-slavery-46][^cite_note-slave-trade-47] Benin's power lasted between the 15th and 19th centuries.[^cite_note-52] Oyo, at its territorial zenith in the late 17th to early 18th centuries, extended its influence from western Nigeria to modern-day [Togo](30138.md).[^cite_note-53]



Depiction of [Benin City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_City) by a Dutch illustrator in 1668. The wall-like structure in the centre probably represents the [walls of Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walls_of_Benin), housing the [Benin bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_bronze) decorated historic [Benin City Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_the_Oba_of_Benin).

In the north, the incessant fighting amongst the Hausa city-states and the decline of the [Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire) allowed the Fulani people to gain headway into the region. Until this point, the Fulani, a [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad) foreign ethnic group, primarily traversed the semi-desert region with cattle. They largely avoided engaging in [trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade), [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture), [state-building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-building), and other activities common among other West African peoples. At the beginning of the 19th century, [Usman dan Fodio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usman_dan_Fodio) led [a successful jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulani_War) against the [Hausa Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms), He accused the Hausa of not being true [Muslims](19541.md) and subsequently established the [Sokoto Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate).[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-\:4-55] The Sokoto Caliphate relied heavily on [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery), with large-scale plantation slavery, concubinage, and domestic servitude. While the primary source of new slaves were the newly conquered Hausa peoples, raids on non-Muslim communities and other ethnic groups accused of not being true Muslims persisted.[^cite_note-\:4-55][^cite_note-Murray2021-56] The caliphate as a whole became one of the largest slave societies in 19th-century Africa. This empire, grew rapidly under his rule and that of [his descendants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Sultans_of_Sokoto), who sent out [invading armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fula_jihads) in every direction. The Sokoto Caliphate sought to link the eastern and western Sudan regions while attempting to expand southward into the [Yoruba country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorubaland). It conquered parts of the old [Oyo Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oyo_Empire) (modern-day [Kwara](2227645.md)), and attempted to penetrate deeper into the heart of Yorubaland. However, the Yoruba forces of [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan) delivered a decisive defeat to the invaders at the [Battle of Osogbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_%C3%92sogbo), halting their jihad and successfully protecting the sovereign integrity of Yoruba territory. The sultan sent out emirs to establish [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty) over the previous conquered territories and promote Islamic civilisation; the emirs in turn became increasingly rich and powerful through trade and slavery. By the 1890s, the largest [slave population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Africa), about two million, was concentrated in the territories of the Sokoto [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate). The use of slave labour was extensive, especially in agriculture.[^cite_note-57] By the time of its break-up in 1903 into various European colonies, the Sokoto Caliphate was one of the largest pre-colonial African states.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]<sup>: 136 </sup>

A changing legal imperative (the outlawing of the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md) in 1807) and economic imperative (a desire for political and social stability) led most European powers to support the widespread cultivation of agricultural products, such as the palm, for use in European industry. The slave trade continued after the ban, as illegal [smugglers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smugglers) purchased slaves along the coast from native slavers. Britain's [West Africa Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Africa_Squadron) sought to intercept the smugglers at sea. The [rescued slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saro_people) were taken to [Freetown](56661.md), a colony in West Africa originally established for the resettlement of slaves freed by Britain.[^cite_note-60]

### British colonisation and independence

Main articles: [Colonial Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Nigeria) and [Federation of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Nigeria)



Flag of the [Lagos Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Colony)

Britain intervened in the Lagos kingship power struggle by bombarding Lagos in 1851, deposing the slave-trade-friendly [Oba Kosoko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosoko), helping to install the amenable Oba [Akitoye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akitoye) and signing the [Treaty between Great Britain and Lagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_Between_Great_Britain_and_Lagos,_1_January_1852) on 1 January 1852. [Britain](31717.md) annexed Lagos as a [crown colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_colony) in August 1861 with the [Lagos Treaty of Cession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Treaty_of_Cession). British missionaries expanded their operations and travelled further inland. In 1864, [Samuel Ajayi Crowther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Ajayi_Crowther) became the first African [bishop of the Anglican Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Nigeria).[^cite_note-61] In 1885, British claims to a West African [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence) received recognition from other European nations at the [Berlin Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Conference). The following year, it chartered the [Royal Niger Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Niger_Company) under the leadership of Sir [George Taubman Goldie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Taubman_Goldie). By the late 19th and early 20th centuries, the company had vastly succeeded in subjugating the independent southern kingdoms along the [Niger River](21437.md), the British conquered [Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_Empire) in 1897, and, in the [Anglo-Aro War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Aro_War) (1901–1902), defeated other opponents. The defeat of these states opened up the Niger area to British rule. In 1900, the company's territory came under the direct control of the British government and established the [Southern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Nigeria_Protectorate) as a British [protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate) and part of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire).[^cite_note-62]



[The Lord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Lugard,_1st_Baron_Lugard) and [Lady Lugard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora_Shaw,_Lady_Lugard), 1908

By 1902, [the British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_British) had begun plans to move north into the Sokoto Caliphate. British General Lord [Frederick Lugard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Lugard,_1st_Baron_Lugard) was tasked by the [Colonial Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Office) to implement the agenda. Lugard used rivalries between many of the emirs in the southern reach of the caliphate and the central Sokoto administration to prevent any defence as he worked towards the capital. As the British approached the city of [Sokoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_\(city\)), Sultan [Muhammadu Attahiru I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Attahiru_I) organised a quick defence of the city and fought the advancing British-led forces. The British force quickly won, sending Attahiru I and thousands of followers on a [Mahdist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahdi) *[hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegira)*. In the northeast, the decline of the [Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire) gave rise to the British-controlled [Borno Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_Emirate), which established [Abubakar Garbai of Borno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Garbai_of_Borno) as ruler.[^cite_note-63]



[Emir of Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Abbas_\(Emir_of_Kano\)) with cavalry, 1911

In 1903, the British victory in the [Battle of Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kano_\(1903\)) gave them a logistical edge in [pacifying the heartland of the Sokoto Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kwatarkwashi) and parts of the former Bornu Empire. On 13 March 1903, at the grand market square of Sokoto, the last [vizier of the caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Grand_Vizier) officially conceded to British rule. The British appointed [Muhammadu Attahiru II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Attahiru_II) as the new caliph. Lugard abolished the caliphate but retained the title *sultan* as a symbolic position in the newly organised [Northern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Nigeria_Protectorate). This remnant became known as "[Sokoto Sultanate Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Sultanate_Council)". In June 1903, the British defeated the remaining northern forces of Attahiru. The caliphate forces in the south continued to resist the British and Germans in the [Adamawa Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamawa_Wars). By 1907, resistance to European rule in the area had been quelled.[^cite_note-64]

On 1 January 1914, the British formally united the [Southern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Nigeria_Protectorate) and the [Northern Nigeria Protectorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Nigeria_Protectorate) into the [Colony and Protectorate of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Nigeria). Administratively, Nigeria remained divided into the Northern and Southern Protectorates and [Lagos Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Colony). Inhabitants of the southern region sustained more interaction, economic and cultural, with the British and other Europeans owing to the coastal economy.[^cite_note-65] Christian missions established Western educational institutions in the protectorates. Under Britain's policy of indirect rule and validation of Islamic legitimist tradition, the Crown did not encourage the operation of Christian missions in the northern, Islamic part of the country.[^cite_note-66]

[Nigerian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_nationalism) first emerged in the 1920s, with its most significant figure being [Herbert Macaulay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Macaulay). His vision aimed to bring all Nigerians together and demand independence.[^cite_note-67] By the mid-20th century, following [World War II](32927.md), a [wave for independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Africa) was sweeping across Africa. In response to the growth of Nigerian nationalism and demands for independence, successive constitutions legislated by the British government moved Nigeria toward self-government on a representative and increasingly federal basis. By the eve of independence in 1960, regional differences in modern educational access were marked. The legacy, though less pronounced, continues to the present day. The balance between north and south was also expressed in Nigeria's political life. For instance, northern Nigeria did not outlaw slavery until 1936 whilst in other parts of Nigeria, slavery was abolished soon after colonialism.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-slave-trade-47]



1953 postage stamp with portrait of Queen [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II)



[Nnamdi Azikiwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nnamdi_Azikiwe), first President of Nigeria (1963–1966)

Nigeria gained a degree of [self-rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-governance) in 1954, and full independence from the United Kingdom on [1 October 1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Nigeria\)), as the [Federation of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Nigeria) with [Abubakar Tafawa Balewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Tafawa_Balewa) as its [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Nigeria), while retaining the [Monarch of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Nigeria_\(1960%E2%80%931963\)), [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II), as nominal head of state and [Queen of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_of_Nigeria). [Nnamdi Azikiwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nnamdi_Azikiwe) replaced the colonial [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-general) in November 1960. At independence, the cultural and political differences were sharp among Nigeria's dominant ethnic groups: the Hausa in the north, Igbo in the east, and Yoruba in the west.[^cite_note-JBS2-69] The [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system) of government was retained, and thus the [President](516088.md)'s powers were generally ceremonial.[^cite_note-70] The parliamentary system of government had Abubakar Tafawa Balewa as Prime Minister and Nnamdi Azikiwe as the ceremonial president. The founding government was a coalition of conservative parties: the [Northern People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_People's_Congress) led by Sir [Ahmadu Bello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadu_Bello), a party dominated by Muslim northerners, and the Igbo and Christian-dominated [National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Nigeria_and_the_Cameroons) led by Nnamdi Azikiwe. The opposition consisted of the comparatively liberal [Action Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Group_\(Nigeria\)), which was largely dominated by the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) and led by [Obafemi Awolowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obafemi_Awolowo). An imbalance was created in the polity as a result of the [1961 plebiscite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_British_Cameroons_referendum). [Southern Cameroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons) opted to join the [Republic of Cameroon](5447.md) while [Northern Cameroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Cameroon) chose to join Nigeria. The northern part of the country became larger than the southern part.

Abubakar Tafawa Balewa

[Abubakar Tafawa Balewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Tafawa_Balewa), Nigeria's first Prime Minister during the 1st Republic&#x20;

### Early republic and civil war

Main articles: [First Nigerian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nigerian_Republic) and [Nigerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War)

At Nigeria's independence in 1960, the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) colonial authorities, distrustful of the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) and [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) peoples, handed disproportionate power to the Northern elites.[^cite_note-\:6-71][^cite_note-\:9-72][^cite_note-\:10-73][^cite_note-\:15-74][^cite_note-\:16-75] In large this was due to the fact that the British had enjoyed far greater cooperation through indirect rule with the Hausa-Fulani aristocracy, who had been far more submissive and offered significantly less resistance than the leaders of the Yoruba and Igbo peoples collectively.[^cite_note-\:6-71][^cite_note-\:9-72][^cite_note-\:10-73][^cite_note-\:15-74][^cite_note-\:16-75] The [Northern Region](2292852.md) gained more seats in parliament than the southerner [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Region,_Nigeria) and [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Region_of_Nigeria) regions combined—this would cement Northern dominance in Nigerian politics for years to come. Resentment among southern politicians precipitated into political chaos in the country. [Obafemi Awolowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obafemi_Awolowo), Premier of Western Region, was accused of attempting to overthrow the government. This followed a period of conflict between the AG regional government and the central government. In spite of the flimsiness of the evidence presented by the government's prosecutors, he was convicted. With incarceration of Awolowo, [Samuel Akintola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Akintola) took over as the premier of Western Region. Because Akintola was an ally of Ahmadu Bello, the undisputed strong man of Nigeria, Akintola was criticised as being a tool of the North.[^cite_note-Jorre,_p._30-76] As premier of the West, Akintola presided over the most chaotic era in Western Region—one which earned it the nickname "[the Wild-Wild West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_West)". However, as late as Thursday, 13 January 1966, Balewa had announced that the federal government was not going to intervene in the West.[^cite_note-Jorre,_p._30-76] However, the very next day, Akintola, premier of the West met with his ally [Ahmadu Bello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadu_Bello), the [Sardauna of Sokoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardauna_of_Sokoto), premier of the North and party boss of NPC party to which Balewa belonged.[^cite_note-Jorre,_p._30-76] At the same time a top-level security conference in Lagos was taking place which was attended by most of the country's senior army officers. All of this activity created rumours that the Balewa government would be forced to crack down on lawlessness in the West using military might.[^cite_note-Jorre,_p._30-76]

&#x20;The disequilibrium and perceived corruption of the electoral and political process led to two [military coups](25914154.md) in 1966. The [first coup was in January 1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and was led mostly by soldiers under Majors [Emmanuel Ifeajuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Ifeajuna) (of the [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) tribe), [Chukwuma Kaduna Nzeogwu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chukwuma_Kaduna_Nzeogwu) ([Northerner](2292852.md) of [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Region,_Nigeria) extraction) and [Adewale Ademoyega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adewale_Ademoyega) (a Yoruba from the West). The coup plotters succeeded in assassinating Sir [Ahmadu Bello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadu_Bello) and Sir [Abubakar Tafawa Balewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Tafawa_Balewa) alongside prominent leaders of the Northern Region and Premier [Samuel Akintola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladoke_Akintola) of the [Western Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_State_\(Nigeria\)), but the plotters struggled to form a central government. Senate President [Nwafor Orizu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nwafor_Orizu) handed over government control to the [Army](14943749.md), under the command of another Igbo officer, Major General[^cite_note-77] [Johnson Aguiyi-Ironsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Aguiyi-Ironsi). Later, the [counter-coup of 1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_Nigerian_counter-coup), supported primarily by Northern military officers, facilitated the rise of [Yakubu Gowon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakubu_Gowon) as military head of state. Tension rose between north and south; [Igbos in northern cities suffered persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_anti-Igbo_pogrom) and many fled to the Eastern Region.[^cite_note-78]



The [Republic of Biafra](44431.md) in June 1967, when it declared its independence from the rest of Nigeria

In May 1967, Governor of the [Eastern Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Region,_Nigeria) Lt. Colonel [Emeka Ojukwu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Odumegwu_Ojukwu) declared the region independent from the federation as a state called the [Republic of Biafra](44431.md) as a result of the continuous and systematically planned attacks against Igbos and those of Eastern extraction, popularly known as the [1966 pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1966_anti-Igbo_pogrom).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] This declaration precipitated the [Nigerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War) which began as the official Nigerian government side attacked Biafra on 6 July 1967, at Garkem. The 30-month war, with a long [blockade of Biafra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Biafra) and its isolation from trade and international relief, ended in January 1970.[^cite_note-DDRS-81] Estimates of the number of dead in the former Eastern Region during the 30-month civil war range from one to three million.[^cite_note-82] The UK and [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) were the main military backers of the Nigerian government, with Nigeria utilising air support from Egyptian pilots provided by [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser),[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] while [France](5843419.md) and [Israel](9282173.md) aided the Biafrans. The [Congolese](76762.md) government under President [Joseph-Désiré Mobutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobutu_Sese_Seko) took an early stand on the Biafran secession, voicing strong support for the Nigerian federal government[^cite_note-85] and deploying thousands of troops to fight against the [secessionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separatist_movements_of_Nigeria).[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87]



The [blockade of Biafra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Biafra) during the [Nigerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War) (1967–1970) resulted in a famine that ultimately cost at least a million lives.

Following the war, Nigeria enjoyed an [oil boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_boom) in the 1970s, during which the country joined [OPEC](166346.md) and received huge oil revenues. Despite these revenues, the military government did little to improve the [standard of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living), help small and medium businesses, or invest in infrastructure. As oil revenues fuelled the rise of federal subsidies to states, the federal government became the centre of political struggle and the threshold of power in the country. As oil production and revenue rose, the Nigerian government became increasingly dependent on oil revenues and international commodity markets for budgetary and economic concerns.[^cite_note-88] The [coup in July 1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), led by Generals [Shehu Musa Yar'Adua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehu_Musa_Yar'Adua) and [Joseph Garba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Nanven_Garba), ousted Gowon,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201142–43Derfler201181-89] who fled to Britain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDerfler201182-90] The coup plotters wanted to replace Gowon's autocratic rule with a triumvirate of three brigadier generals whose decisions could be vetoed by a [Supreme Military Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Military_Council_of_Nigeria_\(1966%E2%80%931979\)). For this triumvirate, they convinced General [Murtala Muhammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtala_Muhammed) to become military head of state, with General [Olusegun Obasanjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olusegun_Obasanjo) as his second-in-command, and General [Theophilus Danjuma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophilus_Danjuma) as the third.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201143Derfler201181-91] Together, the triumvirate introduced austerity measures to stem inflation, established a Corrupt Practices Investigation Bureau, replaced all military governors with new officers, and launched "Operation Deadwood" through which they fired 11,000 officials from the civil service.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201144-92]

Colonel [Buka Suka Dimka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buka_Suka_Dimka) launched a [February 1976 coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt),[^cite_note-93] during which General Murtala Muhammed was assassinated. Dimka lacked widespread support among the military, and his coup failed, forcing him to flee.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201148-94] After the coup attempt, General Olusegun Obasanjo was appointed military head of state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201148–49Derfler201185-95] Obasanjo vowed to continue Murtala's policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIliffe201150Derfler201185-96] Aware of the danger of alienating northern Nigerians, Obasanjo brought General Shehu Yar'Adua as his replacement and second-in-command as [Chief of Staff, Supreme Headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_Nigeria) completing the military triumvirate, with Obasanjo as head of state and General Theophilus Danjuma as [Chief of Army Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_Army_Staff_\(Nigeria\)), the three went on to re-establish control over the [military regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_military_juntas_of_1966%E2%80%9379_and_1983%E2%80%9399) and organised the military's transfer of power programme: [states creation](226734.md) and [national delimitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_boundary_delimitation), [local government reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_areas_of_Nigeria) and the [constitutional drafting committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nigeria#1979_constitution_\(Second_Republic\)) for a new republic.[^cite_note-97]

### Military dictatorship and coups

Main articles: [Military dictatorship in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Nigeria) and [Military coups in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_coups_in_Nigeria)

&#x20;The military carefully planned the return to civilian rule putting in place measures to ensure that political parties had broader support than witnessed during the first republic. In 1979, five political parties competed in a series of elections in which Alhaji [Shehu Shagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehu_Shagari) of the [National Party of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_of_Nigeria) (NPN) was elected president. All five parties won representation in the National Assembly. On 1 October 1979, Shehu Shagari was sworn in as the first [President and Commander-in-Chief](516088.md) of the Federal Republic of Nigeria. Obasanjo peacefully transferred power to Shagari, becoming the first head of state in Nigerian history to willingly step down.[^cite_note-98]



[Shehu Shagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehu_Shagari) was the first elected [President of Nigeria](516088.md) from 1979 to 1983.

In [August 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Nigerian_presidential_election), Shagari and the NPN were returned to power in a landslide victory, with a [majority of seats in the National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Nigerian_parliamentary_election) and control of 12 state governments. However, the elections were marred by violence, and allegations of widespread vote-rigging and electoral malfeasance led to legal battles over the results. There were also uncertainties, such as in the first republic, that political leaders may be unable to govern properly. The [1983 military coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) was coordinated by key officers of the Nigerian military and led to the overthrow of the government and the installation of Major General [Muhammadu Buhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Buhari) as head of state. The military coup of Muhammadu Buhari shortly after the regime's re-election in 1984 was generally viewed as a positive development.[^cite_note-99] [Ibrahim Babangida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Babangida) overthrew Buhari in the [coup d'état of 1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat). Babangida established the [Nigerian Political Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Political_Bureau_of_1986) in 1986 which made recommendations for the transition to the Third Nigerian Republic. In 1989, Babangida started making plans for the transition to the Third Nigerian Republic. Babangida survived the [1990 Nigerian coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt), then postponed a promised return to democracy to 1992.[^cite_note-100]



[Abdulsalami Abubakar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulsalami_Abubakar), military ruler in 1998, saw the return to democracy in 1999.

Babangida legalised the formation of political parties and formed the two-party system with the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(Nigeria\)) and [National Republican Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Republican_Convention) ahead of the [1992 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Nigerian_parliamentary_election). He urged all Nigerians to join either of the parties, which Chief [Bola Ige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bola_Ige) referred to as "two leper hands". The [1993 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Nigerian_presidential_election) held on 12 June was the first since the military coup of 1983. The results, though not officially declared by the National Electoral Commission, showed the duo of [Moshood Abiola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshood_Abiola) and [Baba Gana Kingibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Gana_Kingibe) of the Social Democratic Party defeated [Bashir Tofa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashir_Tofa) and [Sylvester Ugoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvester_Ugoh) of the National Republican Convention by over 2.3 million votes. However, Babangida annulled the elections, leading to massive civilian protests that effectively shut down the country for weeks. In August 1993, Babangida finally kept his promise to relinquish power to a civilian government but not before appointing [Ernest Shonekan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Shonekan) head of an [interim national government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Ernest_Shonekan).[^cite_note-101] Babangida's regime has been considered the most corrupt and responsible for creating a culture of corruption in Nigeria.[^cite_note-102]

Shonekan's interim government, the shortest in the political history of the country, was overthrown in a [coup d'état of 1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Nigerian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) led by General [Sani Abacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sani_Abacha), who used military force on a wide scale to end the crisis of the Third Republic. In 1995, Abacha's regime hanged environmentalist [Ken Saro-Wiwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Saro-Wiwa) on trumped-up charges in the deaths of four [Ogoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ogoni_people) elders, which caused Nigerian's [suspension from the Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suspension_from_the_Commonwealth_of_Nations). Lawsuits under the American [Alien Tort Statute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_Tort_Statute) against [Royal Dutch Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Dutch_Shell) and Brian Anderson, the head of Shell's Nigerian operation, settled out of court.[^cite_note-103] Several hundred million dollars in accounts traced to Abacha were discovered in 1999.[^cite_note-104] The regime came to an end in 1998 when the dictator died in the villa. Abacha looted money to offshore accounts in Western European banks and maintained his grip on power through arrests and bribing generals and politicians. His successor, General [Abdulsalami Abubakar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulsalami_Abubakar), adopted a new constitution on 5 May 1999, which provided for multiparty elections.

### 1999–present

Main article: [Fourth Nigerian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Nigerian_Republic)



[Olusegun Obasanjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olusegun_Obasanjo) served as president of Nigeria from 1999 to 2007.

On 29 May 1999, Abubakar handed over power to the winner of the 1999 presidential election, former military ruler General [Olusegun Obasanjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olusegun_Obasanjo), as President of Nigeria. Obasanjo had been in prison under the dictatorship of Abacha. Obasanjo's inauguration heralded the beginning of the Fourth Nigerian Republic,[^cite_note-105] ending a 39-year period of short-lived democracies, civil war and military dictatorship. Although the elections that brought Obasanjo to power and allowed him to run for a second term in the 2003 presidential elections were condemned as unfree and unfair, Nigeria made significant progress in democratisation under Obasanjo.[^cite_note-106]

In the 2007 general elections, [Umaru Yar'Adua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umaru_Musa_Yar'Adua) of the People's Democratic Party came to power. The international community, which had observed the Nigerian elections to promote a free and fair process, condemned these elections as seriously flawed.[^cite_note-107] Yar'Adua died on 5 May 2010, and Vice President [Goodluck Jonathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodluck_Jonathan) had been sworn in by the Senate three months earlier as acting president to succeed Yar'Adua.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] Jonathan won the 2011 presidential election; the polls went smoothly and with relatively little violence or electoral fraud.[^cite_note-New_York2-110] Jonathan's tenure saw an economic recovery that made Nigeria the leading economic power in Africa.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] The Jonathan administration also saw an unparalleled increase in corruption, with as many as 20 billion US dollars said to have been lost to the Nigerian state through the national oil company. Above all, however, Jonathan's tenure saw the emergence of a wave of terror by the [Boko Haram insurgency](27868253.md), including the [Gwoza massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwoza_massacre) and [Chibok schoolgirls kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibok_schoolgirls_kidnapping) in 2014.[^cite_note-113]



Chief [Bola Tinubu](3034327.md) is serving as President of Nigeria since 29 May 2023.

Ahead of the [general election of 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Nigerian_general_election), a merger of the biggest opposition parties in Nigeria – the [Action Congress of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Congress_of_Nigeria), the [Congress for Progressive Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_for_Progressive_Change), the [All Nigeria Peoples Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Nigeria_Peoples_Party), a faction of the [All Progressives Grand Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Progressives_Grand_Alliance) and the new PDP (a faction of serving governors of the ruling People's Democratic Party) – formed the [All Progressives Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Progressives_Congress) led by current president Bola Ahmed Tinubu. At the time, it was the most expensive election ever to be held on the African continent (being surpassed only by the elections of [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Nigerian_general_election) and [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Nigerian_presidential_election)). The new mega-opposition party chose as their candidate for the election former military dictator Muhammadu Buhari. Buhari's campaign in 2015 was popular and built around his image as a staunch anti-corruption fighter—he won the election by over two million votes. Observers generally praised the election as being fair.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] The election marked the first time an incumbent president had lost re-election in Nigeria. Insecurity heightened drastically under Buhari with [banditry](68366617.md), [insurgency](27868253.md) and [separatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separatist_movements_of_Nigeria) agitations increasingly widespread. The economy also experienced two recessions and global oil shocks as a result of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Nigeria). Buhari was reelected in the [2019 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Nigerian_general_election); he died two years after leaving office.[^cite_note-118]

Four major candidates, amongst other less popular candidates, vied for the presidency in the [2023 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Nigerian_presidential_election). For the first time since the return of democracy, no former military ruler ran for president, marking a strengthening of democracy and faith in the [multiparty constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nigeria). The election also saw the rise of metonymic supporters of the new candidates, the [Obidient movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obidient_Movement) of [Peter Obi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Obi), previously governor of Anambra State, widely appealed to young, urban voters and has his core base in the Southeast;[^cite_note-119] and the Kwankwassiya of [Rabiu Kwankwaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabiu_Kwankwaso), former governor of [Kano State](2123717.md) in the Northwest.[^cite_note-120]

[Bola Tinubu](3034327.md) of the ruling party won the disputed election with 36.61% of the vote,[^cite_note-121] though both runners-up claimed victory and litigation continued in an election tribunal.[^cite_note-122] Tinubu was [inaugurated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Bola_Tinubu) on 29 May 2023.[^cite_note-123] His government faced ongoing domestic challenges, including widespread [kidnapping in Nigeria](62987269.md).[^cite_note-124] On 29 May 2024, Tinubu signed a law reinstating *[Nigeria, We Hail Thee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria,_We_Hail_Thee)*, the national anthem from 1960 to 1978, replacing *[Arise, O Compatriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arise,_O_Compatriots)*. The [United States carried out a strike](83205369.md) against [Islamic State](46529902.md) militants in [northwest Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_\(Nigeria\)) on 25 December 2025 at the request of Tinubu's government, marking the first foreign military intervention in post-independence Nigeria.[^cite_note-125] U.S. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) stated the strike was meant to counter [alleged violence against Christians](43330495.md) in the north. In May 2026, a joint military campaign executed by American and Nigerian forces in northeastern Nigeria resulted in the elimination of Abu-Bilal al-Minuki, the global second-in-command of the Islamic State. In July, the United States withdrew most of its active personnel from the country and shifted its operational focus to providing intelligence support.[^cite_note-126]

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Nigeria), [Climate change in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Nigeria), [Geology of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Nigeria), and [Wildlife of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Nigeria)



Topography of Nigeria

Nigeria is located in western Africa on the [Gulf of Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Guinea) and has a total area of 923,768 km<sup>2</sup> (356,669 mi<sup>2</sup>),[^cite_note-127] making it the world's [31st-largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area). Its borders span 4,047 kilometres (2,515 mi), and it shares borders with Benin (773 km or 480 mi), Niger (1,497 km or 930 mi), Chad (87 km or 54 mi), and Cameroon (including the separatist [Ambazonia](841514.md)) 1,690 km or 1,050 mi. Its [coastline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastline) is at least 853 km (530 mi).[^cite_note-128] Nigeria lies between latitudes [4°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_parallel_north) and [14°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_parallel_north), and longitudes [2°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_meridian_east) and [15°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_meridian_east). The highest point in Nigeria is [Chappal Waddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chappal_Waddi) at 2,419 m (7,936 ft). The main rivers are the [Niger](21437.md) and the [Benue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benue_River), which converge and empty into the [Niger Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_Delta). This is one of the world's largest river deltas and the location of a large area of Central African [mangroves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove).

Nigeria's most expansive topographical region is that of the valleys of the Niger and Benue river valleys (which merge and form a Y-shape).[^cite_note-encarta22-129] To the southwest of the Niger is a "rugged" [highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highland_\(geography\)). To the southeast of the Benue are hills and mountains, which form the [Mambilla Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mambilla_Plateau), the highest plateau in Nigeria. This plateau extends through the border with [Cameroon](5447.md), where the [montane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montane) land is part of the [Bamenda Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda_Highlands) of Cameroon.



Climate map of Nigeria

The far south is defined by its [tropical rainforest climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate), where annual rainfall is 1,500 to 2,000 millimetres (60 to 80 in) per year.[^cite_note-gai52-130] In the southeast stands the [Obudu Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obudu_Plateau). [Coastal plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_plain) are found in both the southwest and the southeast.[^cite_note-encarta22-129] [Mangrove swamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove_swamp) are found along the coast.[^cite_note-gai42-131]

The area near the border with Cameroon close to the coast is rich rainforest and part of the [Cross-Sanaga-Bioko coastal forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross%E2%80%93Sanaga%E2%80%93Bioko_coastal_forests) [ecoregion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion), an important centre for [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity). It is a habitat for the [drill primate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drill_\(animal\)), which is found in the wild only in this area and across the border in Cameroon. The areas surrounding [Calabar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calabar), Cross River State, also in this forest, are believed to contain the world's largest diversity of butterflies. The area of southern Nigeria between the Niger and the [Cross Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_River_\(Nigeria\)) has lost most of its forest because of development and harvesting by increased population and has been [replaced by grassland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross%E2%80%93Niger_transition_forests).

&#x20;Everything in between the far south and the far north is [savannah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna) (insignificant tree cover, with grasses and flowers located between trees). Rainfall is more limited to between 500 and 1,500 millimetres (20 and 60 in) per year.[^cite_note-gai52-130] The savannah zone's three categories are [Guinean forest-savanna mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinean_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic), Sudan savannah, and Sahel savannah.[^cite_note-gai42-131]



Shrinking of [Lake Chad](59314.md) in north-eastern Nigeria, with the outline of the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles) for size comparison

### Hydrology

Nigeria is divided into two main catchment areas – that of Lake Chad and that of the Niger. The Niger catchment area covers about 63% of the country. The main tributary of the Niger is the Benue, whose tributaries extend beyond Cameroon into Cameroon into Chad and the Sharie catchment area. In the Sahel region, rain is less than 500 millimetres (20 in) per year, and the [Sahara Desert](325363.md) is encroaching.[^cite_note-gai52-130] In the dry northeast corner of the country lies [Lake Chad](59314.md), on a [shared water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad_Basin_Commission) boundary [delimitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_boundary_delimitation) with Niger, Chad and Cameroon.

The Chad Basin is fed from the north-eastern quarter of Nigeria. The Bauchi Plateau forms the watershed between the Niger/Benue and Komadugu Yobe river systems. The flat plains of north-eastern Nigeria are geographically part of the Chad Basin, where the course of the El Beid River forms the border with Cameroon, from the Mandara Mountains to Lake Chad. The Komadugu Yobe river system gives rise to the internationally important Hadejia-Nguru wetlands and Ox-bow lakes around Lake Nguru in the rainy season.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] Other rivers of the northeast include the Ngadda and the Yedseram, both of which flow through the Sambisa swamps, thus forming a river system. The river system of the northeast is also a major river system.[^cite_note-134] In addition, Nigeria has numerous coastal rivers.



Photo of Lake Chad from [Apollo 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_7), 1968

Over the last million years, [Lake Chad](59314.md) in the far north-east of Nigeria has dried up several times for a few thousand years and just as often growing to many times its current size. In recent decades its surface area has been reduced considerably, which may also be due to humans taking water from the inlets to irrigate agricultural land.[^cite_note-135]

### Vegetation

Nigeria is covered by three types of vegetation: forests (where there is significant tree cover), [savannahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savannah) (insignificant tree cover, with grasses and flowers located between trees), and [montane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montane) land (least common and mainly found in the mountains near the Cameroon border). Both the forest zone and the savannah zone are divided into three parts.[^cite_note-gai42-131]

Some of the forest zone's most southerly portion, especially around the Niger River and [Cross River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_River_\(Nigeria\)) deltas, is [mangrove swamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_mangroves). North of this is a freshwater swamp, containing different vegetation from the saltwater mangrove swamps, and north of that is a rainforest.[^cite_note-gai42-131]

The savannah zone's three categories are divided into [Guinean forest-savanna mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinean_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic), made up of plains of tall grass which are interrupted by trees, the most common across the country; Sudan savannah, with short grasses and short trees; and [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel) savannah patches of grass and sand, found in the northeast.[^cite_note-gai42-131]



The [Mambilla Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mambilla_Plateau) in the North-Eastern region of Nigeria

### Environmental issues

Further information: [Deforestation in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Nigeria) and [Environmental issues in the Niger Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_the_Niger_Delta)

Nigerian deforrestation 1981 – 2020

Deforestation in Nigeria 1981–2020

[^cite_note-136]

[Waste management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_management) including [sewage treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewage_treatment), the linked processes of [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) and [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_retrogression_and_degradation), and [climate change](5042951.md) are the major environmental problems in Nigeria.[^cite_note-137]

**Climate change**

This section is an excerpt from [Climate change in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Nigeria).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Nigeria\&action=edit)]



Satellite image of [Lake Chad](59314.md), showing it shrinking between 1984 and 2018



Temperature change in Nigeria; each bar represents the average temperature over that year.

[Climate change in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Nigeria) has caused increasing temperatures and [rainfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainfall) variability (increasing in coastal areas and declining in continental areas) resulting in [drought](37335.md), [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification), rising sea levels, erosion, floods, [thunderstorms](70807.md), [bush fires](56106.md), [landslides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landslides), [land degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_degradation) and more frequent, extreme weather conditions.[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Nigeria_\:8-138] [Climate change](5042951.md) is leading to [biodiversity loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_loss), reduced food and water security, increasing poverty, conflict, [displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_displacement), economic instability and negative health outcomes in Nigeria.[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Nigeria_\:10-139] Nigeria is highly vulnerable to and not well prepared to deal with the effects of climate change.[^cite_note-140] The agricultural sector is particularly most vulnerable.[^cite_note-141]



A thick layer of [Harmattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmattan) fog in [Enugu State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enugu_State)

Nigeria is in the top 25 highest [greenhouse gas](21350772.md) emitters, contributing 0.8% of the global total [emissions.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_intensity)[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Nigeria_\:9-142] Nigeria has committed to cut [greenhouse](21350772.md) gas emissions by 20% on its own, and by 47% if it receives international support, by 2030. The country has also committed to [net zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net-zero_emissions) by 2060.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] Nigeria's climate change [mitigation](2119179.md) and [adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation) plans focus on agriculture and food security (through e.g.: [climate-smart agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate-smart_agriculture)), forests and [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity), water resources, energy and infrastructure (e.g.: transitioning to [renewable energies](25784.md) like [solar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_energy)), health, human settlement, [industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_\(economics\)) and [commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce), transportation and communication.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] While there is some discussion about necessary [capacity](3045799.md) building at the individual, group and community level to engage in climate change responses, there is less attention given to higher levels of capacity building at the state and national level.[^cite_note-Climate_change_in_Nigeria_Haider-147]

**Deforestation**

In 2005, Nigeria had the highest rate of [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) in the world, according to the [Food and Agriculture Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization) of the [United Nations](31769.md).[^cite_note-148] That year, 12.2%, the equivalent of 11,089,000 hectares, had been forested in the country. Between 1990 and 2000, Nigeria lost an average of 409,700 hectares of forest every year equal to an average annual deforestation rate of 2.4%. Between 1990 and 2005, in total Nigeria lost 35.7% of its [forest cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover) or around 6,145,000 hectares.[^cite_note-149] Nigeria had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 6.2/10, ranking it 82nd globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-150]

**Pollution**

Waste management poses problems in a [megacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megacity) like Lagos and other major Nigerian cities, which are linked to economic development, population growth, and the inability of municipal councils to manage the resulting rise in industrial and domestic waste. This waste management problem is also attributable to unsustainable environmental management lifestyles of [Kubwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kubwa,_Abuja) community in the Federal Capital Territory, where there are habits of indiscriminate disposal of waste, dumping of waste along or into the canals, sewerage systems that are channels for water flows, and the like. Haphazard industrial planning, increased urbanisation, poverty, and the lack of competence of the municipal government are seen as the major reasons for high levels of waste pollution in the country's major cities. Some of the solutions have been disastrous to the environment, resulting in untreated waste being dumped in places where it can pollute waterways and [groundwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groundwater_in_Nigeria).[^cite_note-151]

[Nigeria's Delta region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_Delta) is one of the most polluted regions in the world due to serious [oil spills](158705.md) and other environmental problems caused by its oil industry.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] The heavy contamination of the air, ground and water with toxic pollutants is often used as an example of [ecocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecocide).[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] In additional to the environmental damage it has caused [conflict in the Delta region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_in_the_Niger_Delta).

Illegal oil refineries, in which local operators convert stolen crude oil into petrol and diesel, are considered particularly "dirty, dangerous and lucrative".[^cite_note-159] Safety and environmental aspects are usually ignored. Refining petroleum also inevitably produces heavy oil, which is "cracked" into lighter fuel components in regular plants at great technical expense. Illegal refineries do not have these technical capabilities and "dispose" of the heavy oil where it then accumulates. The lighter components of crude oil ([methane](18582230.md) to [butane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butane), [isobutane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isobutane)) create a certain risk of explosion, which often leads to disasters at illegal plants.[^cite_note-160] In 2022, Nigeria suffered 125 deaths from explosions at local, illegal refineries.[^cite_note-161]

In 2010, thousands of people were inadvertently [exposed to lead-containing soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamfara_State_lead_poisoning_outbreak) from informal [gold mining](1291393.md) within the northern state of Zamfara. While estimates vary, it is thought that upwards of 400 children died of acute [lead poisoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_poisoning).[^cite_note-162]

## Politics

Main articles: [Politics of Nigeria](21387.md), [Political parties in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_parties_in_Nigeria), and [Elections in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Nigeria)

### Government



[Coat of arms of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Nigeria) in current use

Nigeria is a [federal republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic) modelled after the [United States](3434750.md),[^cite_note-163] with 36 states and the capital Abuja as an independent unit. The executive power is exercised by the [President](516088.md). The president is both [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) and [head of the federal government](37570.md). The president is [elected by popular vote](652563.md) to a maximum of two four-year terms.[^cite_note-factbook3-164] [State governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_governors_in_nigeria?action=edit\&redlink=1), like the president, are elected for four years and may serve a maximum of two terms. The president's power is limited by a [Senate](3610871.md) and a [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Nigeria\)), which are combined in a [bicameral body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) called the [National Assembly](1898990.md). The Senate is a 109-seat body with three members from each state and one from the capital region of Abuja; members are elected by popular vote to four-year terms. The House contains 360 seats, with the number of seats per state determined by population.[^cite_note-factbook3-164]

The Nigerian president is elected in a modified two-round system. To be elected in the first round, a candidate must receive a majority of the votes and more than 25% of the votes in at least 24 of the 36 states.[^cite_note-165] If no candidate reaches this hurdle, a second round of voting takes place between the leading candidate and the next candidate who received the majority of votes in the highest number of states. By convention, presidential candidates take a running mate (candidate for the vice presidency) who is both ethnically and religiously the opposite of themselves. There is no law prescribing this, yet all presidential candidates since the existence of the Fourth Republic until 2023 adhered to this rule.

However, this principle of religious and ethnic diversity in leadership was ignored in the 2023 General Elections, where the candidate for the [All Progressives Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Progressives_Congress), [Bola Ahmed Tinubu](3034327.md), a Muslim, selected another Muslim, [Senator Kashim Shettima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashim_Shettima), as running mate.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Subdivisions of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Nigeria)



Map of Nigeria with administrative divisions

Nigeria is divided into [thirty-six states](226734.md) and one [Federal Capital Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Capital_Territory,_Nigeria), which are further sub-divided into 774 [local government areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_areas_of_Nigeria). In some contexts, the states are aggregated into [six geopolitical zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geopolitical_zones_of_Nigeria): [North West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_\(Nigeria\)), [North East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_\(Nigeria\)), [North Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Central,_Nigeria), [South West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_\(Nigeria\)), [South East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_East_\(Nigeria\)), and [South South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_South).[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]

Nigeria has five [cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nigerian_cities_by_population) with a population of over a million (from largest to smallest): [Lagos](85232.md), [Kano](508782.md), [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan), [Benin City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin_City) and [Port Harcourt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Harcourt). Lagos is the [largest city in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_populous_cities_in_Africa), with a population of over [12 million](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_by_population) in its [urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area).[^cite_note-felix2-170]

The south of the country in particular is characterised by very strong urbanisation and a relatively large number of cities. According to an estimate from 2015,[^cite_note-171] there are 20 cities in Nigeria with more than 500,000 inhabitants, including ten cities with a population of one million.

### Law

Main article: [Law of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Nigeria)

The [Constitution of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nigeria) is the supreme law of the country. There are four distinct legal systems in Nigeria, which include [English law](85441.md), [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law), [customary law](218798.md), and [Sharia law](28840.md):

- English law in Nigeria consists of the collection of British laws from colonial times.

- Common law is the collection of authoritative judicial decisions in the field of civil law (so-called precedents) that have been handed down in the country concerned – in this case Nigeria. (This system is mainly found in Anglo-Saxon countries; in continental Europe, on the other hand, codified and, as far as possible, abstracted civil law predominates, as in the [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code) in France).

  [^cite_note-172]

- Customary law is derived from indigenous traditional norms and practices, including the dispute resolution meetings of pre-colonial Yoruba land secret societies and the [Èkpè](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egbo) and Okónkò of [Igboland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igboland) and [Ibibioland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibibio_people).

  [^cite_note-173]

- [Sharia in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_in_Nigeria) *(also known as Islamic Law)* used to be used only in [Northern Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Nigeria), where [Islam](6037917.md) is the predominant [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion). It is also being used in [Lagos State](626168.md), Oyo State, Kwara State, Ogun State, and Osun State by Muslims. Muslim penal codes are not the same in every state and they differentiate in punishment and offences according to religious affiliation (for example, alcohol consumption and distribution).

The country has a [judicial branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_branch), the highest court of which is the [Supreme Court of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Nigeria).[^cite_note-174]

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Nigeria](21392.md)



The Ministry of [Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Affairs), [Abuja](56823.md)

Upon gaining independence in 1960, Nigeria made African unity the centrepiece of its foreign policy.[^cite_note-175] One exception to the African focus was Nigeria's close relationship with Israel throughout the 1960s. Israel sponsored and oversaw the construction of Nigeria's parliament buildings.[^cite_note-176]

Nigeria's foreign policy was put to the test in the 1970s after the country emerged united from its civil war.
