It supported movements against white minority governments in [Southern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Africa). Nigeria backed the [African National Congress](2503.md) by taking a committed tough line about the South African government. Nigeria was a founding member of the [Organisation for African Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_African_Unity) (now the [African Union](10634933.md)) and had tremendous influence in West Africa and Africa on the whole. Nigeria played a leading role in regional integration in West Africa, partnering with neighboring states to spearhead the creation of the [Economic Community of West African States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States) (ECOWAS) in 1975.[^cite_note-ECOWAS_History_Official-177] It subsequently became the primary military and financial backbone—acting as the regional standard-bearer—for the ECOWAS Monitoring Group ([ECOMOG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECOMOG)), leading peacekeeping and intervention efforts during the civil wars in Liberia and Sierra Leone.[^cite_note-US_State_Dept_ECOWAS-178][^cite_note-179]

With this Africa-centred stance, Nigeria readily [sent troops to the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_Crisis) at the behest of the United Nations shortly after independence (and has maintained membership since that time). Nigeria also supported several Pan-African and pro-self-government causes in the 1970s, including garnering support for [Angola](701.md)'s [MPLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Movement_for_the_Liberation_of_Angola), [SWAPO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SWAPO) in Namibia, and aiding opposition to the minority governments of [Portuguese Mozambique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozambican_War_of_Independence), and [Rhodesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodesian_Bush_War). Nigeria retains membership in the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement). In late November 2006, it organised an Africa-South America Summit in [Abuja](56823.md) to promote what some attendees termed "South-South" linkages on a variety of fronts.[^cite_note-180] Nigeria is also a member of the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) and the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations). It was temporarily expelled from the latter in 1995 when ruled by the [Abacha regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sani_Abacha).

Nigeria has remained a key player in the [international oil industry](994887.md) since the 1970s and maintains membership in [OPEC](166346.md), which it joined in July 1971. Its [status as a major petroleum producer](3542988.md) figures prominently in its sometimes volatile international relations with [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country), notably the United States, and with developing countries.[^cite_note-181]

The [Agence Française de Développement (AFD)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Development_Agency) supports large infrastructure and environmental projects in Nigeria.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] Since 2000, [Chinese–Nigerian trade relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Nigeria_relations) have risen exponentially. There has been an increase in total trade of over 10.3 billion dollars between the two nations from 2000 to 2016.[^cite_note-Taylor_Pathology_of_Dependency-184] The [CCECC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Civil_Engineering_Construction_Corporation) and [CRRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRRC) have also financed infrastructure projects (rail network, ports, airports) in Nigeria.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] However, the structure of the Chinese–Nigerian trade relationship has become a major political issue for the Nigerian state. Chinese exports account for around 80 per cent of total bilateral trade volumes.[^cite_note-Taylor_Pathology_of_Dependency-184] This has resulted in a serious [trade imbalance](4810.md), with Nigeria importing ten times more than it exports to China.[^cite_note-Taylor_Pathology_of_Dependency-184] Subsequently, Nigeria's economy is becoming over-reliant on cheap imports to sustain itself, resulting in a clear decline in Nigerian industry under such arrangements.[^cite_note-Taylor_Pathology_of_Dependency-184]

Continuing its Africa-centred foreign policy, Nigeria introduced the idea of a [single currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Currency_union) for West Africa known as the [Eco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eco_\(currency\)) under the presumption that it would be led by the [naira](178686.md). But on 21 December 2019, [Ivorian President](384272.md) [Alassane Ouattara](1173624.md), [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md), and multiple other [UEMOA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States) states announced that they would merely rename the [CFA franc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CFA_franc) instead of replacing the currency as originally intended.[^cite_note-188] As of 2021, the Eco currency has been delayed to 2027.[^cite_note-189]

### Military

Main article: [Nigerian Armed Forces](21391.md)



[Nigerian Army](14943749.md) self-propelled anti-aircraft gun

The [Nigerian Armed Forces](21391.md) are the [combined military forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military) of Nigeria. The Nigerian military is the largest and widely recognised as the strongest military in [Sub-Saharan-Africa](27067.md), ranking third overall on the [African continent](5334607.md), only behind [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Algeria](358.md).[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] It consists of three uniformed service branches: the [Nigerian Army](14943749.md), [Nigerian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Navy), and [Nigerian Air Force](538869.md). The [President of Nigeria](516088.md) functions as the [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the armed forces, exercising his [constitutional authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nigeria) through the Ministry of Defence, which is responsible for the management of the military and its personnel. The operational head of the AFN is the [Chief of the Defence Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Defence_Staff_\(Nigeria\)), who is subordinate to the [Nigerian Defence Minister](9815893.md). With a force of more than 223,000 active personnel, the Nigerian military is one of the largest uniformed combat services in Africa.[^cite_note-192]



[Nigerian Air Force](538869.md) [Mi-24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi-24) attack helicopter

Nigeria had 143,000 troops in the armed forces (army 100,000, navy 25,000, air force 18,000) and another 80,000 personnel for "gendarmerie & paramilitary" in 2020, according to the [International Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Strategic_Studies).[^cite_note-\:8-193] It spent just under 0.4 per cent of its economic output, or US\$1.6 billion, on its armed forces in 2017.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] In 2022, it budgeted US\$2.26 billion for the armed forces.[^cite_note-\:8-193]

### Communal conflicts

Main articles: [Communal conflicts in Nigeria](54964174.md), [Religious violence in Nigeria](43330495.md), and [Nigerian bandit conflict](68366617.md)



Attacks by Boko Haram, 2011 to October 2022. Each figure represents 1,000 deaths.

[^cite_note-\:5-196]

The predominantly [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_people)-led [Boko Haram](23769768.md) and the [bandit conflict](68366617.md) have been responsible for numerous serious attacks with thousands of casualties since mid-2010. Since then, according to the Council on Foreign Relations' Nigeria Security Tracker, over 43,200 lives have been lost to this conflict (as of June 2023).[^cite_note-\:5-196] The United Nations refugee agency UNHCR counts about 1.8 million internally displaced persons and about 200,000 Nigerian refugees in neighbouring countries.

The Boko Haram-affected states agreed in February 2015 to establish an 8,700-strong Multinational Joint Task Force to jointly fight Boko Haram. By October 2015, Boko Haram had been driven out of all the cities it controlled and almost all the counties in northeastern Nigeria. In 2016, Boko Haram split and in 2022, 40,000 fighters surrendered.[^cite_note-197] The splinter group [ISWAP](46529902.md) (Islamic State in West Africa) remains active.

The fight against Boko Haram, other sectarians, and criminals, has been accompanied by increasing police attacks. The [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations)' Nigeria Security Tracker counted 1,086 deaths from Boko Haram attacks and 290 deaths from police violence in the first 12 months of its establishment in May 2011. In the 12 months after October 2021, 2,193 people died from police violence and 498 from Boko Haram and ISWAP,[^cite_note-\:5-196] according to the NST. The Nigerian police are notorious for [vigilante justice](58325.md).[^cite_note-\:5-196]

The Niger Delta saw intense [attacks on oil infrastructure in 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Niger_Delta_conflict) by militant groups such as the *Movement for the Emancipation of the Niger Delta* (MEND), the *Niger Delta People's Volunteer Force* (NDPVF), the *Ijaw National Congress* (INC) and the *Pan Niger Delta Forum* (PANDEF). In response, the new Buhari government pursued a dual strategy of repression and negotiation.

In late 2016, the Nigerian federal government resorted to the gambit of offering the militant groups a 4.5 billion naira (US\$144 million) contract to *guard* oil infrastructure. Most accepted. The contract was [renewed in August 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tompolo#Arrest_warrant), but led to fierce disputes among the above-mentioned groups over the distribution of the funds. Representatives speak of "war"[^cite_note-\:7-198] – against each other. The high propensity for violence and the pettiness of the leaders, as well as the complete absence of social and environmental arguments in this dispute[^cite_note-\:7-198] give rise to fears that the militant groups, despite their lofty names, have discarded responsibility for their region and ethnic groups and have moved into the realm of protection rackets and self-enrichment. In any case, the pipelines in the Niger Delta are not very effectively "guarded" – the pollution of the Niger Delta with stolen crude oil and [illegally produced heavy fuel oil](#Oil_spills) continued unhindered after 2016.[^cite_note-199]

In central Nigeria, [conflicts between Muslim Fulani herders and indigenous Christian farmers](54964174.md) flared up again, especially in Kaduna, Plateau, Taraba and Benue states. In individual cases, these clashes have claimed several hundred lives. Conflict over land and resources is increasing due to the ongoing desertification in northern Nigeria, population growth and the generally tense economic situation.[^cite_note-200]

In June 2022, a massacre took place in the St. Francis Xavier Church, in Owo. The Government blamed ISWAP for the murder of over 50 parishioners, but locals suspect Fulani herdsmen's involvement.[^cite_note-201]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Nigeria)



[Lagos](85232.md), the financial centre of Nigeria

Nigeria's economy is the [third largest in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)), the [46th-largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) by nominal GDP, and [19th-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity). As of 2023, Nigeria's economy is classified as lower-middle-income.[^cite_note-202] Before 1999, economic development was hindered by years of military rule, corruption, and mismanagement. In the decades thereafter, the restoration of democracy and subsequent economic reforms led to rapid growth. In 2011, [Citigroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citigroup) projected that Nigeria would have the highest average GDP growth in the world between 2010 and 2050.[^cite_note-203]

In April 2026, [FTSE Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FTSE_Russell) upgraded Nigeria from ‘unclassified’ to ‘frontier market’.[^cite_note-204] [Moody’s](1705862.md)[^cite_note-205] and [S\&P](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S\&P_Global_Ratings)[^cite_note-206] upgraded Nigeria in their rankings in 2025 and 2026. Five out of seven African tech [unicorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicorn_\(finance\)) are based in Nigeria,[^cite_note-207] and just under a third of all African tech start-ups originate from Nigeria.[^cite_note-208] The metropolis of Lagos is the leading financial centre in West and Central Africa. Nigeria accounts for 28 per cent of all African fintech companies and is a leading hub for venture capital (VC) funding, having attracted the highest share of funding for African fintech companies – around 36 per cent – between 2020 and the first half of 2024.[^cite_note-209] On 14 September 2026, the Nigerian stock market witnessed the largest initial public offering (IPO) in African history, that of the Dangote Refinery.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]

Alongside [Ghana](12067.md) and [Angola](701.md), Nigeria was among the African countries with the highest trade surpluses in 2025.[^cite_note-213] In early 2026, Nigeria recorded its highest quarterly trade surplus since its foundation in 1914.[^cite_note-214] The [World Bank](36752.md) forecasts that Nigeria will achieve its highest economic growth in the last 10 years in 2026/2027.[^cite_note-215]

Nigeria is an economic leader in Africa in several industries, including energy, [financial markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_market), [pharmaceuticals](180121.md), and [entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entertainment_industry). Its [financial services sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Nigeria) is well-developed, with a mix of local and international banks, asset management companies, brokerage houses, insurance companies and brokers, private equity funds, and investment banks.[^cite_note-216] After petroleum, the largest source of foreign exchange earnings for Nigeria are [remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance) sent home by Nigerians living abroad.[^cite_note-tribune.com.ng2-217]

Nigeria also has an abundant supply of under-exploited natural resources, including coal, [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite), [tantalite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tantalite), gold, [tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin), iron ore, [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone), [niobium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niobium), lead and [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc).[^cite_note-218] The country's gold production in 2015 was 8 metric tons.[^cite_note-219] Despite huge deposits of these natural resources, the mining industry in Nigeria is still in its infancy.[^cite_note-220]

### Agriculture

Further information: [Agriculture in Nigeria](5215046.md)



Nigerian palm nuts put out to dry

In 2021, about 23.4% of Nigeria's GDP was contributed by agriculture, forestry and fishing combined.[^cite_note-221] Nigeria is the world's largest producer of [cassava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassava_production_in_Nigeria).[^cite_note-\:2-222] Additional major crops include [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet), [yam beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yam_\(vegetable\)), and [guinea corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum_bicolor) (sorghum).[^cite_note-223] [Cocoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean) is the principal agricultural export, and one of the country's most significant non-petroleum products.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] Nigeria is also one of the world's top twenty exporters of [natural rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber), generating \$20.9 million in 2019.[^cite_note-226]

Before the [Nigerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War) and the [oil boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_boom), Nigeria was self-sufficient in food,[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-\:11-228][^cite_note-229] and agriculture was the principal foreign exchange earner.[^cite_note-230] Though the Nigerian government promoted the use of inorganic fertilisers in the 1970s,[^cite_note-pasquini2-231] agriculture failed to keep pace with population growth and Nigeria now relies upon food imports to feed its people.[^cite_note-\:11-228][^cite_note-232] In 2022, it was spending US\$6.7 billion yearly for food imports, four times its income from food exports.[^cite_note-\:2-222]

Nigeria's rice production increased by 10% from 2017/18 to 2021/22, reaching 5 million tonnes per year,[^cite_note-233] but it failed to keep up with rising demand. Consequently, rice imports remained steady at 2 million tonnes annually. In August 2019, Nigeria closed its border with Benin and other neighbouring countries to stop rice smuggling into the country as part of efforts to boost local production.[^cite_note-234]

Nigeria exports unhusked rice while relying on imports for processed husked rice, the country's staple food. The [rice mill in Imota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imota_rice_mill), near Lagos, was opened in 2023 to handle processing domestically, improve the balance of trade, bolster the labour market, and reduce transport and middleman costs, with design targets to support 250,000 direct and indirect jobs and produce 2.5 million 50-kg bags of rice annually. However, severe shortages of raw paddy, security disruptions, and high logistics costs have prevented the facility from reaching capacity, keeping Nigeria dependent on foreign rice imports.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-GuardianImota2025-236]

### Oil and natural gas

Further information: [Petroleum industry in Nigeria](3542988.md) and [Oil theft in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_theft_in_Nigeria)

Nigeria is the [15th largest producer of petroleum in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production), the [6th largest exporter](17808102.md), and has the [9th largest proven reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves). Petroleum plays a large role in the Nigerian economy and politics, accounting for about 80% of government earnings. Nigeria also has the [9th largest proven natural gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves) estimated by [OPEC](166346.md); the government's value of its about 206.53 trillion cubic feet has been valued at \$803.4 trillion.[^cite_note-237] Natural gas is seen as having the potential to unlock an [economic miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_miracle) on the Niger River.[^cite_note-238] Nigeria each year loses to [gas flaring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_flare) an estimate of US\$2.5 billion,[^cite_note-239] and over 120,000 barrels of oil per day to [crude theft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_theft_in_Nigeria) in the [Niger Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_Delta), its main oil-producing region.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] This has led to [piracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piracy_in_the_Gulf_of_Guinea) and [conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_in_the_Niger_Delta) for control in the region and has led to disruptions in production preventing the country from meeting its OPEC quota and exporting petroleum at full capability.[^cite_note-242]



Overflight photo of the creeks of the Niger Delta

Nigeria has a total of 159 [oil fields](2910801.md) and 1,481 [wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_well) in operation according to the [Department of Petroleum Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Petroleum_Resources).[^cite_note-NDES2-243] The most productive region of the nation is the coastal [Niger Delta Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_Delta_Basin_\(geology\)) in the Niger Delta or "south-south" region which encompasses 78 of the 159 oil fields. Most of Nigeria's oil fields are small and scattered, and as of 1990, these small fields accounted for 62.1% of all Nigerian production. This contrasts with the sixteen largest fields which produced 37.9% of Nigeria's petroleum at that time.[^cite_note-politicaleconomy2-244] Petrol was Nigeria's main import commodity until 2021, accounting for 24% of import volume.[^cite_note-245]

The Niger Delta Nembe Creek oil field was discovered in 1973 and produces from the middle [Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) [deltaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_delta) [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone)-[shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shale) in an [anticline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticline) [structural trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_trap) at a depth of 2 to 4 kilometres (7,000 to 13,000 feet).[^cite_note-246] In June 2013, Shell announced a strategic review of its operations in Nigeria, hinting that assets could be divested. While many international oil companies have operated there for decades, by 2014 most were making moves to divest their interests, citing a range of issues including oil theft. In August 2014, Shell said it was finalising its interests in four Nigerian oil fields.[^cite_note-ShellNigeria2-247]

The supply of natural gas to Europe, threatened by the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md), pushed projects to transport Nigerian natural gas via pipelines to Morocco or Algeria.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] As of May 2022, however, there are no results on this yet.

Thanks to the success of the [Dangote refinery](58744636.md), which opened in 2023, Nigeria is transforming from an oil exporter into a fuel and fertiliser exporter.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Nigeria)



[Kainji Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kainji_Dam) on the [Niger River](21437.md), built in the 1960s

Nigeria's energy consumption is much more than its generation capacity. Most of the energy comes from traditional fossil fuels, which account for 73% of total primary production. The rest is from hydropower (27%). Since independence, Nigeria has tried to develop a domestic nuclear industry for energy. Nigeria opened 2004 a Chinese-origin research reactor at [Ahmadu Bello University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadu_Bello_University) and has sought the support of the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md) to develop plans for up to 4,000 MW of nuclear capacity by 2027 according to the National Program for the Deployment of Nuclear Power for Generation of Electricity. In 2007, President [Umaru Yar'Adua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umaru_Musa_Yar'Adua) urged the country to embrace nuclear power to meet its growing energy needs. In 2017, Nigeria signed the UN [Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Prohibition_of_Nuclear_Weapons).[^cite_note-253] In April 2015, Nigeria began talks with Russia's state-owned [Rosatom](1641369.md) to collaborate on the design, construction and operation of four nuclear power plants by 2035, the first of which will be in operation by 2025. In June 2015, Nigeria selected two sites for the planned construction of the nuclear plants. Neither the Nigerian government nor Rosatom would disclose the specific locations of the sites, but it is believed that the nuclear plants will be sited in [Akwa Ibom State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akwa_Ibom_State) and [Kogi State](299399.md). The sites are planned to house two plants each. In 2017 agreements were signed for the construction of the [Itu nuclear power plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itu_nuclear_power_plant).

#### Electricity

According to the survey, 94% of Nigerians are connected to the national grid, but only 57% have their electricity consumption recorded by an electricity meter.[^cite_note-\:12-254] Only 1% of Nigerians surveyed reported having electricity 24 hours a day. 68% have electricity 1 to 9 hours a day, according to the NIO. Two-thirds of Nigerians, or 66%, pay up to 10,000 Naira (US\$13) a month for electricity, which is almost 3% of the average income in Nigeria.[^cite_note-\:12-254] Over two-thirds of respondents, or 67%, were willing to pay more for uninterrupted electricity supply. Power generators are owned by 21% of Nigerians, while 14% use solar energy.[^cite_note-\:12-254]

### Manufacturing and technology

Further information: [Automotive industry in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Nigeria) and [Pharmaceutical industry in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_Nigeria)



[Nigeria EduSat-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria_EduSat-1), the first satellite built by Nigeria by the [Federal University of Technology Akure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Technology_Akure)

Nigeria has a manufacturing industry that includes leather and textiles (centred in [Kano](508782.md), [Abeokuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abeokuta), [Onitsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onitsha), and Lagos), plastics and processed food. [Ogun](1436438.md) is considered to be Nigeria's current industrial hub, as most factories are located in Ogun and more companies are moving there, followed by Lagos.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257] The city of [Aba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aba,_Abia) in the south-eastern part of the country is well known for handicrafts and shoes, known as "Aba made".[^cite_note-Naijalitz-258] Nigeria has a market of 720,000 cars per year, but less than 20% of these are produced domestically.[^cite_note-\:1-259]

In 2016, Nigeria was the [leading cement producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement_production_by_country) south of the Sahara, ahead of South Africa.[^cite_note-260] [Aliko Dangote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aliko_Dangote), Nigeria's richest inhabitant, based his wealth on cement production, as well as agricultural commodities.[^cite_note-261] According to its own information, the [Ajaokuta Steel Company Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajaokuta_Steel_Mill) produces 1.3 million tonnes of [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel) per year.[^cite_note-262] However, steel plants in [Katsina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsina_\(city\)), [Jos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos) and [Osogbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osogbo) no longer appear to be active.[^cite_note-263]

In June 2019, [Nigeria EduSat-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria_EduSat-1) was deployed from the [International Space Station](15043.md). It is the first satellite that was built in Nigeria, which followed [many other Nigerian satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_weather_and_communications_satellites) that were built by other countries.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-\:3-265] In 2021, Nigeria hosts about 60 percent of the pharmaceutical production capacity in Africa,[^cite_note-266] the larger pharmaceutical companies are located in [Lagos](85232.md).[^cite_note-267] The pharmaceutical producer with the most employees in Nigeria is [Emzor Pharmaceutical Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emzor_Pharmaceutical_Industries?action=edit\&redlink=1) Ltd.[^cite_note-268] Nigeria has a few electronic manufacturers like [Zinox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinox?action=edit\&redlink=1), the first branded Nigerian computer, and manufacturers of electronic gadgets such as tablet PCs.[^cite_note-269] As of January 2022, Nigeria is the host to 5 out of the 7 [unicorn companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unicorn_\(company\)) in Africa.[^cite_note-270]

### Internet and telecommunications

Main article: [Telecommunications in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Nigeria)



Nigerian librarians editing the [Wikidata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikidata) database

&#x20;The Nigerian telecommunications market is one of the fastest-growing in the world, with major emerging market operators (like [MTN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTN_Group), [9mobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9mobile?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Airtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airtel) and [Globacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globacom)) basing their largest and most profitable centres in the country.[^cite_note-271] Nigeria's ICT sector has experienced much growth, representing 10% of the nation's GDP in 2018 as compared to just 1% in 2001.[^cite_note-272] Lagos is regarded as one of the largest technology hubs in Africa with its thriving tech ecosystem.[^cite_note-273] According to a survey by the [GSM Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GSM_Association), 92% of adult Nigerian men and 88% of women owned a mobile phone.[^cite_note-punch-274] Using various measures including but not limited to illegal arrest, taking down of websites, passport seizures, and restricted access to bank accounts, the [Nigerian government is punishing citizens for expressing themselves on the internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Nigeria#Internet_censorship_and_surveillance) and working to stifle internet freedom.[^cite_note-275]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Nigeria)



[Owu waterfalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owu_waterfalls), visited by Nigerian [undergraduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undergraduate_education)

Tourism in Nigeria centres largely on events, because of the country's ample amount of ethnic groups, but also includes rain forests, savannahs, waterfalls, and other natural attractions.[^cite_note-suntra2-276] Abuja is home to several parks and green areas. The largest, [Millennium Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennium_Park_\(Abuja\)), was designed by architect [Manfredi Nicoletti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manfredi_Nicoletti) and officially opened in December 2003. After the re-modernisation project achieved by the administration of Governor Raji Babatunde Fashola, Lagos is gradually becoming a major tourist destination. Lagos is currently taking steps to become a [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city). The 2009 Eyo carnival (a yearly festival originating from [Iperu Remo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iperu,_Ogun), Ogun State) was a step toward world city status. Currently, Lagos is primarily known as a business-oriented and fast-paced community.[^cite_note-METRO2-277] Lagos has become an important location for African and black cultural identity.[^cite_note-278]

Lagos has sandy beaches by the Atlantic Ocean, including [Elegushi Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elegushi_Beach) and Alpha Beach. Lagos also has many private beach resorts including Inagbe Grand Beach Resort and several others in the outskirts. Lagos has a variety of hotels ranging from three-star to five-star hotels, with a mixture of local hotels such as [Eko Hotels and Suites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eko_Hotels_and_Suites), [Federal Palace Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Palace_Hotel) and franchises of multinational chains such as [Intercontinental Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/InterContinental), [Sheraton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheraton_Hotels_and_Resorts), and [Four Points by Sheraton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Points_by_Sheraton). Other places of interest include the [Tafawa Balewa Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tafawa_Balewa_Square), Festac town, The [Nike Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nike_Art_Gallery), [Freedom Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Park_\(Lagos\)), and the [Cathedral Church of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Church_of_Christ,_Lagos).

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Nigeria)

Due to Nigeria's location in the centre of West Africa, [transport plays a major role in the national service sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Nigeria). Government investments have seen an increase in extensive road repairs and new construction has been carried out gradually as states in particular spend their share of increased government allocations. Representative of these improvements is the [Second Niger Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Niger_bridge) near Onitsha, which was largely completed in 2022.[^cite_note-279] A 2017 World Bank report on logistics hubs in Africa placed the country in fourth place, behind Côte d'Ivoire, Senegal, and Sao Tome,[^cite_note-280] but in 2021, Nigeria joined the World Logistics Passport, a private sector group working to increase the efficiency of global trade.[^cite_note-281]

#### Roads

Main article: [Roads in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Nigeria)



Map of trans-African automobile routes

Four trans-African automobile routes pass through Nigeria:

-  [Lagos-Mombasa Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos-Mombasa_Highway)

-  [Algiers-Lagos Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers-Lagos_Highway)

-  [Dakar-Lagos Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakar-Lagos_Highway)

-  [Dakar-Ndjamena Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakar-Ndjamena_Highway)

Nigeria has the largest road network in [West Africa](67393.md). It covers about 200,000 km, of which 60,000 km are asphalted. Nigeria's roads and highways handle 90% of all passenger and freight traffic. It contributes N2.4trn (\$6.4bn) to GDP in 2020. The federal government is responsible for 35,000 km of the road network. The motorway links of important economic centres such as [Lagos](85232.md)-[Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan), Lagos-[Badagry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badagry) and [Enugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enugu_\(city\))-[Onitsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onitsha) have been renovated.[^cite_note-282]

The rest of the road network is a state matter and therefore in very different shape, depending on the state. Economically strong states such as Lagos, [Anambra](905838.md) and [Rivers](1966992.md) receive particularly poor evaluations.[^cite_note-283] Most roads were built in the 1980s and early 1990s. Poor maintenance and inferior materials have worsened the condition of the roads. Travelling is very difficult. Especially during the rainy season, the use of secondary roads is sometimes almost impossible due to potholes.[^cite_note-284] Road bandits often take advantage of this situation for their criminal purposes.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]

#### Rail transport

Main article: [Rail transport in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Nigeria)

Railways have undergone a massive revamping with projects such as the [Lagos-Kano Standard Gauge Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos%E2%80%93Kano_Standard_Gauge_Railway) being completed connecting cities of [Kano](2123717.md), [Kaduna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaduna_\(city\)), [Abuja](56823.md), [Ibadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadan) and [Lagos](85232.md). [Lagos Rail Mass Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_Rail_Mass_Transit) controls transport in Lagos with two currently operating services: the [Red Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_\(Lagos_Transit\)) and the [Blue Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Line_\(Lagos_Transit\)). [Abuja Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuja_Light_Rail) controls rail services in Abuja and currently has one operating service, the Blue Line.

#### Air transport

Main article: [List of airports in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Nigeria)



An A340-500 of [Arik Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arik_Air)&#x20;

The Nigerian aviation industry generated 198.62 billion naira (€400 million) in 2019, representing a contribution of 0.14% to GDP. It was the fastest-growing sector of the Nigerian economy in 2019. Passenger traffic increased from 9,358,166 in 2020 to 15,886,955 in 2021, a significant increase of over 69%. Aircraft movements increased by more than 46% from 2020 to 2021. Total freight volumes were 191 tonnes in 2020 but increased to 391 tonnes in 2021.[^cite_note-287] In December 2021, the [Anambra International Cargo Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinua_Achebe_International_Airport) started its operation.[^cite_note-288] In April 2022, the second terminal of the [Murtala Muhammed International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtala_Muhammed_International_Airport) has been inaugurated. It will increase the capacity of the airport to 14 million passengers per year.[^cite_note-289]



B737-300 of [Air Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Peace)

There are 54 airports in Nigeria, The principal airports are:[^cite_note-290]

- [Murtala Muhammed International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murtala_Muhammed_International_Airport) in Lagos,

- [Nnamdi Azikiwe International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nnamdi_Azikiwe_International_Airport) in Abuja,

- [Mallam Aminu Kano International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallam_Aminu_Kano_International_Airport) in Kano,

- [Akanu Ibiam International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akanu_Ibiam_International_Airport) in Enugu and

- [Port Harcourt International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Harcourt_International_Airport) in Port Harcourt.

Nigeria had in the past operated a state-owned airline [Nigeria Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria_Airways) which was over-indebted in 2003 and was bought by the British [Virgin Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Group); since 28 June 2005, it has flown under the name [Virgin Nigeria Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Nigeria). At the end of 2008, the Virgin Group announced its withdrawal from the airline; since September 2009 the airline has been operating as Nigerian Eagle Airlines. The largest airline in Nigeria is privately owned [Air Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Peace), founded in 2012.

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Nigeria](21386.md)

See also: [Social class in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class_in_Nigeria)



Population density (persons per square kilometre) in Nigeria

The [United Nations](31769.md) estimates that the population of Nigeria in 2021 was 213,401,323.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-291][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-292] It was distributed as 51.7% rural and 48.3% urban, and with a population density of 167.5 people per square kilometre. Around 42.5% of the population were 14 years or younger, 19.6% were aged 15–24, 30.7% were aged 25–54, 4.0% were aged 55–64, and 3.1% were aged 65 years or older. The median age in 2017 was 18.4 years.[^cite_note-People_and_Society:_Population22-293] Nigeria is the [world's sixth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population#Sovereign_states_and_dependencies_by_population). The birth rate is 35.2 births/1,000 population and the death rate is 9.6 deaths/1,000 population as of 2017, while the total fertility rate is 5.07 children born/woman.[^cite_note-People_and_Society:_Population22-293] Nigeria's population increased by 57 million from 1990 to 2008, a 60% growth rate in less than two decades.[^cite_note-IEApop20112-294] Nigeria is the most populous country in Africa[^cite_note-295] and accounts for about 17% of the continent's total population as of 2017; however, exactly how populous is a subject of speculation.[^cite_note-auto12-296]



Historical population of Nigeria (1950–2020)

Millions of Nigerians have emigrated during times of economic hardship, primarily to Europe, North America and Australia. It is estimated that over a million Nigerians have emigrated to the United States and constitute the [Nigerian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_American) populace. Individuals in many such Diasporic communities have joined the "Egbe Omo Yoruba" society, a national association of Yoruba descendants in North America.[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] Nigeria's largest city is [Lagos](85232.md). Lagos has grown from about 300,000 in 1950[^cite_note-299] to an estimated 13.4 million in 2017.[^cite_note-Major_Urban_Areas:_Population-300]

Nigeria has more than 250 ethnic groups, with varying languages and customs, creating a country of rich ethnic diversity. The three largest ethnic groups are the [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_people), [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) and [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people), together accounting for more than 60% of the population, while the [Edo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_people), [Ijaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijaw_people), [Fulɓe](102383.md), [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_people), [Urhobo-Isoko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urhobo_people), [Ibibio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibibio_people), [Ebira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebira_people), [Nupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nupe_people), [Gbagyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gbagyi_people), [Jukun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jukun_people_\(West_Africa\)), [Igala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igala_people), [Idoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idoma_people), [Ogoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ogoni_people) and [Tiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiv_people) account for between 35 and 40%; other minorities make up the remaining 5%.[^cite_note-NGeo-301] The [Middle Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Belt) of Nigeria is known for its diversity of ethnic groups, including the [Atyap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atyap_people), [Berom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berom_people), Goemai, Igala, [Kofyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kofyar_people), Pyem, and Tiv.[^cite_note-felix2-170][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] There are small minorities of British, American, [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-resident_Indian_and_person_of_Indian_origin), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_people_in_Nigeria) (est. 50,000),[^cite_note-304] [white Zimbabwean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Zimbabweans),[^cite_note-305] Japanese, Greek, Syrian and [Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Nigerians) immigrants. Immigrants also include those from other West African or East African nations.

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Nigeria)



Map of Nigeria's linguistic groups

The major languages spoken in Nigeria represent three major families of [languages of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Africa): the majority are [Niger-Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Congo_languages) languages, such as [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_language), [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_language), [Ibibio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibibio_language), [Ijaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijaw_languages), [Fulfulde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fula_language), [Ogoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ogoni_languages), and [Edo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_language). [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_language), spoken in the northeast, primarily in [Borno](2227628.md) and [Yobe State](2139960.md), is part of the [Nilo-Saharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilo-Saharan_languages) family, and [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language) is an [Afroasiatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages) language. Some of the largest of these have derived standardised languages from several different dialects and are widely spoken by their respective ethnic groups. The official language of Nigeria, English, was chosen to facilitate the cultural and linguistic unity of the country, owing to the influence of British colonisation which ended in 1960. [Nigerian Pidgin English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Pidgin), first used by British and [African slavers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Africa) to facilitate the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md) in the late 17th century,[^cite_note-306] has replaced the native language for many Nigerians. Of the 525 known languages that have been spoken in Nigeria at some time in its history, eight are now extinct.[^cite_note-307] Nigerian Pidgin English, often known simply as "[Pidgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pidgin)" or "Broken" (Broken English), became a popular [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca), though with varying regional influences on dialect and slang. It is widely spoken within the Niger Delta Region.[^cite_note-308]

In some areas of Nigeria, ethnic groups speak more than one language. Many French speakers from surrounding countries have influenced the English spoken in the border regions of Nigeria and some Nigerian citizens have become fluent enough in French to work in the surrounding countries.[^cite_note-309] Despite the official declaration of French as the second official language in 1996 by General [Sani Abacha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sani_Abacha), the policy has remained largely theoretical. Inadequate qualified teachers, lack of sustained political will, and the dominance of English have prevented the country from becoming fully bilingual.[^cite_note-o351-310][^cite_note-d227-311]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Nigeria)



Predominant religion by Nigerian state according to the 2022 Afrobarometer survey



Nigerian states that implement [some form of sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_in_Nigeria) (in green)

Nigeria is a Muslim-majority country with a sizeable Christian minority, as well as a tiny minority of adherents of [traditional African religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_African_religions) and other religions.[^cite_note-Cia_19-312] The Christian share of Nigeria's population is in decline because of the lower [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) compared to Muslims in the country.[^cite_note-313] As in other parts of Africa where Islam and Christianity are dominant, religious [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) with the traditional African religions is common.[^cite_note-314]

A 2012 report on religion and public life by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) stated that in 2010, 49.3% of Nigeria's population was Christian, 48.8% was Muslim, and 1.9% were followers of indigenous and other religions (such as the [Bori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_animism) in the North) or unaffiliated.[^cite_note-Pew_Forum_on_Religion2-315] However, in a report released by Pew Research Center in 2015, the Muslim population was estimated to be 50%, and by 2060, according to the report, Muslims will account for about 60% of the country.[^cite_note-316] The 2010 census of [Association of Religion Data Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Religion_Data_Archives) has also reported that 48.8% of the total population was Christian, slightly larger than the Muslim population of 43.4%, while 7.5% were members of other religions.[^cite_note-auto2-317] However, these estimates should be taken with caution because sample data is mostly collected from major urban areas in the south, which are predominantly Christian.[^cite_note-Regional_Distribution_of_Christians2-318][^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] According to a 2018 estimate in The [World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Factbook) by the [CIA](5183633.md), the population is estimated to be 53.5% Muslim, 45.9% Christian (10.6% Catholic and 35.3% Protestant and other Christian), and 0.6% as other.[^cite_note-321] As of 2020, the Pew Research Center reported that Nigeria's Muslim majority numbered around 56.1% while the country's Christian share had declined to 43%.[^cite_note-\:22-322]

Islam dominates northwestern Nigeria and northeastern Nigeria (Kanuri, Fulani and other groups). In the west, the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_people) people are predominantly Christian with a significant Muslim minority in addition to a few adherents of traditional religions.[^cite_note-323] [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and locally cultivated Christianity are widely practised in Western areas, while [Catholicism](606848.md) is a more prominent Christian feature of southeastern Nigeria. Both Roman Catholicism and Protestantism are observed in the Ibibio, [Efik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efik_people), [Ijo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ijaw_people) and [Ogoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ogoni_people) lands of the south. The [Igbos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people) (predominant in the east) and the [Ibibio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibibio_people) (south) are 98% Christian, with 2% practising traditional religions.[^cite_note-324] Charismatic religious groups such as the [Pentecostal churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Pentecostalism) (18 per cent of the population) play a major role.[^cite_note-325] The [middle belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Belt) of Nigeria contains the largest number of minority ethnic groups in Nigeria, who were found to be majority Christians and members of traditional religions, with a significant Muslim minority.[^cite_note-326]

### Health

Further information: [Health in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Nigeria)



Paediatric ward, General hospital, Ilorin

Health care delivery in Nigeria is a concurrent responsibility of the three tiers of government in the country, and the private sector.[^cite_note-327] Nigeria has been reorganising its health system since the [Bamako Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamako_Initiative) of 1987, which formally promoted community-based methods of increasing accessibility of drugs and health care services to the population, in part by implementing user fees.[^cite_note-328] The new strategy dramatically increased accessibility through community-based health care reform, resulting in more efficient and equitable provision of services. A comprehensive approach strategy was extended to all areas of health care, with subsequent improvement in the health care indicators and improvement in health care efficiency and cost.[^cite_note-329]

Almost half of Nigerians, or 48%, report that they or a household member has fallen ill in the last three months. [Malaria](20423.md) had been diagnosed in 88% of the cases and [typhoid fever](31596.md) in 32%.[^cite_note-\:13-330] High blood pressure was in third place with 8%. For symptoms of malaria, 41% of Nigerians turn to a [hospital](18994221.md), 22% to a chemist's shop, 21% to a pharmacy and 11% seek cure through herbs.[^cite_note-\:13-330] [Tuberculosis](30653.md) (TB) is a significant public health issue in Nigeria, ranking among the top countries with high TB burdens globally.[^cite_note-331]



[Polio vaccination](9901652.md) in [Adamawa State](905832.md), northeastern Nigeria, 2014

The [HIV/AIDS rate in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_Nigeria) is much lower than in other African nations such as Botswana or South Africa whose prevalence (percentage) rates are in the double digits. As of 2019, the [HIV](14170.md) prevalence rate among adults of ages 15–49 was 1.5 per cent.[^cite_note-\:0-332] [Life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) in Nigeria is 54.7 years on average,[^cite_note-\:0-332] and 71% and 39% of the population have access to improved water sources and improved [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation), respectively.[^cite_note-333] As of 2019, the infant mortality is 74.2 deaths per 1,000 [live births](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live_birth_\(human\)).[^cite_note-334]

In 2012, a new bone marrow donor program was launched by the [University of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Nigeria) to help people with [leukaemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leukaemia), [lymphoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymphoma), or [sickle cell disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sickle_cell_disease) to find a compatible donor for a life-saving [bone marrow transplant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hematopoietic_stem_cell_transplantation), which cures them of their conditions. Nigeria became the second African country to have successfully carried out this surgery.[^cite_note-McNeil-335] In the [2014 Ebola outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_African_Ebola_virus_epidemic), Nigeria was the first country to effectively contain and eliminate the Ebola threat that was ravaging three other countries in the West African region; the unique method of [contact tracing](1724851.md) employed by Nigeria became an effective method later used by countries such as the United States when Ebola threats were discovered.[^cite_note-Matt_Schiavenza-336][^cite_note-The_Punch-337][^cite_note-Vanguard-338]

The Nigerian health care system is continuously faced with a shortage of doctors known as "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight)", because of emigration by skilled Nigerian doctors to North America and Europe. In 1995, an estimated 21,000 Nigerian doctors were practising in the United States alone, which is about the same as the number of doctors working in the Nigerian public service. Retaining these expensively trained professionals has been identified as one of the goals of the government.[^cite_note-339]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Nigeria)



Abisogun Leigh Science Building, for the [Lagos State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_State_University)'s Faculty of Science

Education in Nigeria is overseen by the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Ministry_of_Education_\(Nigeria\)). [Local authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_areas_of_Nigeria) take responsibility for implementing policy for state-controlled public education and state schools at a regional level. The education system is divided into [kindergarten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindergarten), [primary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_education), [secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) and [tertiary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education). After the 1970s oil boom, tertiary education was improved so it would reach every subregion of Nigeria. 68% of the Nigerian population is literate, and the rate for men (75.7%) is higher than that for women (60.6%).[^cite_note-CP2006-340]

Nigeria provides free, government-supported education, but attendance is not compulsory at any level, and certain groups, such as nomads and the handicapped, are under-served. Nearly 10.5 million Nigerian children aged 5–14 years are not in school. Only 61% of 6–11 year-olds regularly attend primary school.[^cite_note-341] The education system consists of six years of primary school, three years of junior secondary school, three years of senior secondary school, and four, five or six years of university education leading to a bachelor's degree.[^cite_note-CP2006-340] The government has majority control of university education. Tertiary education in Nigeria consists of universities (public and private), polytechnics, monotechnics, and colleges of education. The country has a total of 138 universities, with 40 federally owned, 39 state-owned, and 59 privately owned. Nigeria was ranked 105th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343]

### Crime

Main articles: [Crime in Nigeria](27740659.md) and [List of massacres in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_in_Nigeria)



A [Nigerian police officer](23794123.md) at the [Eyo festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyo_festival) in Lagos

The security situation in Nigeria is considered inadequate despite political stability. 68% of Nigerians feel "not safe" in their country. 77% do not know of an alarm number ("helpline") for emergencies.[^cite_note-\:14-344] Nigerians, according to the above survey, fear being robbed (24%) or kidnapped (also 24%), being victims of armed bandits or of petty theft (both 8%), or being harmed in the herdsmen-farmers conflict (also 8%).[^cite_note-\:14-344] This is followed by "ritual killings" (4%) and "Boko Haram" (3.5%). Respondents see "more security personnel and better training" (37%), "reduction of unemployment" (13%) and "prayers / divine intervention" (8%) as promising countermeasures.[^cite_note-\:14-344]



Homicides by Nigerian state per year and per 1 million inhabitants, comparing the UK and Turkey (source: Nigeria Security Tracker 1/2020-6/2023)

Spatial data from 2006 to 2021 indicates that [Borno State](2227628.md) recorded the highest absolute and per-capita violent mortality rate in Nigeria, followed by heavily impacted states such as [Lagos State](626168.md), [Kaduna State](2227637.md), and [Zamfara State](2227664.md). In contrast, states such as [Jigawa State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jigawa_State), [Ekiti State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekiti_State), [Kano State](2123717.md), and [Sokoto State](2227660.md) maintained the lowest relative rates of violence-related fatalities per 100,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-345] Insurgencies, communal disputes, and criminal networks increasingly concentrate in vulnerable rural areas. These conflicts are fueled by underlying drivers such as resource scarcity, climate change, weak state governance, ethnic tensions, and high youth unemployment, while being further aggravated by armed groups and conflict entrepreneurs engaging in illicit enterprises like kidnapping for ransom, extortion, illegal mining, and arms trafficking.[^cite_note-346]

There is some [piracy in the Gulf of Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piracy_in_the_Gulf_of_Guinea), with attacks directed at all types of vessels. However, security measures on board of mentioned vessels have recently meant that pirates are now more likely to attack fishing villages.[^cite_note-347]

Internationally, Nigeria is infamous for a type of [advance-fee scam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advance-fee_scam) along with a form of [confidence trick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_trick). The victim is talked into sending money or bank account information to the scammer on the premise that a larger amount of money will be transferred to them. In reality, the scammer collects money from the victim with no payout occurring.[^cite_note-348] In 2003, the Nigerian [Economic and Financial Crimes Commission](6816435.md) was created to combat this and other forms of organised financial crime.[^cite_note-349] The EFCC is quite active.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

### Poverty

Main article: [Poverty in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Nigeria)



The total expenditure of food and non-food produce a poverty incidence of 60.2 percent or 89,096,000 Nigerians living in [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty). This measure is used for poverty headcount comparison across countries. Poverty Line is N54,401.16.

According to the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), 32% of Nigeria's population lives in extreme poverty (as of 2017), living on less than US\$2.15 a day.[^cite_note-353] The [World Bank](36752.md) stated in March 2022 that the number of poor Nigerians had increased by 5 million to 95.1 million during the Covid period.[^cite_note-354] Accordingly, 40% of Nigerians live below the poverty line of US\$1.90 as handled by the World Bank.[^cite_note-355]

The poverty rate may be overestimated due to a lack of information on the extremely huge [informal sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_sector) of the economy.[^cite_note-356] Despite the undoubted existence of slums in Nigeria, for example, the fact that 92% of men and 88% of women in Nigeria own a mobile phone[^cite_note-punch-274] is difficult to reconcile with the poverty percentages published by the IMF and the World Bank.

### Human rights

Main articles: [Human rights in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Nigeria) and [LGBT rights in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Nigeria)



[End SARS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_SARS) is a decentralised social movement and series of mass protests against [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) in Nigeria.

Nigeria's human rights record remains poor.[^cite_note-StateDeptHumanRights2-357] According to the U.S. Department of State,[^cite_note-StateDeptHumanRights2-357] the most significant human rights problems are the use of excessive force by security forces, impunity for abuses by security forces, arbitrary arrests, prolonged pretrial detention, judicial corruption and executive influence on the judiciary, rape, torture and other cruel, [inhuman or degrading treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inhuman_or_degrading_treatment) of prisoners, detainees and suspects; harsh and life‑threatening prison and detention centre conditions; human trafficking for prostitution and forced labour, societal violence and vigilante killings, [child labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_labour_in_Nigeria), child abuse and [child sexual exploitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_sexual_abuse_in_Nigeria), [domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_in_Nigeria), discrimination based on ethnicity, region and religion.

Nigeria is a state party of the [Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Discrimination_Against_Women).[^cite_note-358] It also has signed the [Maputo Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maputo_Protocol), an international treaty on women's rights, and the African Union Women's Rights Framework.[^cite_note-359] Discrimination based on sex is a significant human rights issue. Forced marriages are common.[^cite_note-360] [Child marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage_in_Nigeria) remains common in Northern Nigeria;[^cite_note-361] 39% of girls are married before age 15, although the Marriage Rights Act banning marriage of girls under 18 was introduced on a federal level in 2008.[^cite_note-362] There is rampant [polygamy in Northern Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygamy_in_Nigeria).[^cite_note-363] [Domestic violence is common](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_in_Nigeria). Women have fewer land rights.[^cite_note-364] [Maternal mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_death) was at 814 per 100,000 live births in 2015.[^cite_note-365] [Female genital mutilation is common](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female_genital_mutilation_in_Nigeria), although a ban was implemented in 2015.[^cite_note-366] At least half a million suffer from [vaginal fistula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urogenital_fistula), largely as a result of lack of medical care.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

[Women face a large amount of inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality_in_Nigeria) politically in Nigeria, being subjugated to a bias that is [sexist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexism) and reinforced by socio-cultural, economic and oppressive ways.[^cite_note-Ajayi,_Kunle_200723-369] Women throughout the country were only politically [emancipated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation) in 1979.[^cite_note-370] Yet husbands continue to dictate the votes for many women, which upholds the patriarchal system.[^cite_note-Ajayi,_Kunle_200723-369] Most workers in the [informal sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy) are women.[^cite_note-371] Women's representation in government since independence from Britain is very poor. Women have been reduced to sideline roles in appointive posts throughout all levels of government and still make up a tiny minority of elected officials.[^cite_note-Ajayi,_Kunle_200723-369] But nowadays with more education available to the public, Nigerian women are taking steps to have more active roles in the public, and with the help of different initiatives, more businesses are being started by women.

Under the [Shari'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_in_Nigeria) penal code that applies to Muslims in twelve northern states, offences such as alcohol consumption, [homosexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Nigeria),[^cite_note-372] infidelity and theft carry harsh sentences, including amputation, lashing, stoning and long prison terms.[^cite_note-UKtravel2-373] The local religious police, the Hisbah, enforce Sharia law. Despite this potentially orthodox stance, no one has been executed in Nigeria on the orders of an Islamic Sharia court since 2002.[^cite_note-374]

Nigeria is considered to be one of the most [homophobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia) countries in the world.[^cite_note-pewglobal.org2-375][^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] Nigeria’s [anti-LGBT laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same_Sex_Marriage_\(Prohibition\)_Act_2013) are among the most draconian in the world and criminalise not only queer people, but also their ‘supporters’,[^cite_note-378] such as family members and close friends.[^cite_note-379] These laws enjoy the support of a large majority of the population. In a 2018 poll, 96 per cent of Nigerians said they were opposed to same-sex relationships (although 75 per cent of them were not aware of any such relationships within their personal circle).[^cite_note-380]

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Nigeria), [Mass media in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Nigeria), and [Censorship in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Nigeria)

### Architecture

Main articles: [Architecture of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Nigeria), [Hausa architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_architecture), [Yoruba architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_architecture), [Igbo architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_architecture), and [List of palaces in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_palaces_in_Nigeria)



The palace of the [Bauchi Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_Emirate).



One of the main entrance of the Zaria Palace, Hausa architecture.



The Nsude pyramids, a remarkable example of Igbo architecture, taken in 1935.

Prior to European colonization, indigenous architectural styles across Nigeria adapted local climate conditions, available resources, and social structures through the use of sun-dried mud bricks (*tubali*), timber framing, and thatched roofing. In northern regions, traditional Hausa architecture was integrated into extensive walled urban centers accessed via fortified gatehouses. These residential compounds contained specialized functional areas, including individual sleeping quarters (*daki*), dedicated kitchens (*darki girki*), open shaded shelters (*rumfa*), quarters for unmarried adult males (*shago*), central water wells (*rijiya*), and decorative roof parapet projections (*zanko*). Following the spread of Islam, spatial layouts were modified to include male reception areas (*azure*) to enforce gender segregation and maintain privacy from external visitors.

In southeastern Nigeria, pre-colonial Igbo architecture featured round mud-walled dwellings (*ulo aja oto*) with pitched thatched roofs (*aju* or *atani*) constructed over woven bamboo frameworks and supported by timber beams. Directed by a titled family elder (*okpara*), the single-entrance compound functioned as a cohesive social unit centered around a symbolic meeting hall (*obi*), ancestral shrines (*okwu-alusi*), and individual residential spaces (*ime ulo*). Similarly, traditional Yoruba architecture was characterized by expansive extended-family compounds (*agbo'le*), named after the compound head and constructed through communal labor. These structures utilized thick mud walls finished with sand plaster, termite-resistant timber rafters, and large central courtyards (*orowa*) that accommodated shared domestic activities, livestock, and storage.[^cite_note-381]

### Literature

Main article: [Nigerian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_literature)



[Chinua Achebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinua_Achebe), winner [Booker Prize](4446.md) 2007 and [Peace Award of the German book trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedenspreis_des_Deutschen_Buchhandels) 2002

Most Nigerian literature is written in [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language),[^cite_note-382] partly because this language is understood by most Nigerians. Literature in the [Yoruba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_language), [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language) and [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_language) languages (the three most populous language groups in Nigeria) does exist, however, and in the case of the Hausa, for example, can look back on a centuries-old tradition. [Wole Soyinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wole_Soyinka) won the [Nobel Prize for literature](23385442.md) in 1986. [Ben Okri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Okri) won the prestigious [Booker Prize](4446.md) in 1991; [Chinua Achebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinua_Achebe) did the same in 2007. Achebe also won the [Peace Award of the German Book Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedenspreis_des_Deutschen_Buchhandels) in 2002. [Lola Shoneyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lola_Shoneyin) has won several awards for her book [The Secret Lives of Baba Segi's Wives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Secret_Lives_of_Baba_Segi's_Wives_\(novel\)).

### Music

Main article: [Music of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Nigeria)

The earliest known form of popular music in Nigeria was the [palm-wine music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm-wine_music) which dominated the music landscape in the 1920s. [Tunde King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunde_King) was a prominent name in the genre.[^cite_note-theconversation-383][^cite_note-sunng-384]

The 1930s saw the emergence of Onitsha Native Orchestra. They explored various social themes and trends in their native singing style.[^cite_note-theconversation-383][^cite_note-sunng-384]

In the 1950s and 1960s, [Highlife music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlife) became a popular staple in the country with regional genres such as the [Igbo Highlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_highlife). A notable exponent of the genre were the genre's first Nigerian [boy band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_band) [Oriental Brothers International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Brothers_International), [Bobby Benson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Benson), [Osita Osadebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Stephen_Osita_Osadebe), [Victor Olaiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Olaiya), [Rex Lawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Lawson), [Dr Sir Warrior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr_Sir_Warrior) and [Oliver De Coque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_De_Coque).[^cite_note-theconversation-383][^cite_note-sunng-384]

The 1970s was the era of [Fela Kuti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fela_Kuti), the pioneer of Afrobeat genre – fused from [Highlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlife), [Jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) and [Yoruba Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_music). Fela later evolved into social activism and black consciousness.[^cite_note-theconversation-383][^cite_note-sunng-384]

In the 1980s, [King Sunny Ade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Sunny_Ad%C3%A9) achieved success with [Juju Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B9j%C3%BA_music). Another prominent singer of the era is [William Onyeabor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Onyeabor), who is known for his fusion of [Funk Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk) and [Disco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disco).[^cite_note-theconversation-383][^cite_note-385]

By the 1990s, reggae music transitioned into the music scene. Prominent reggae artiste of the era was [Majek Fashek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majek_Fashek). By the mid-1990s, [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop) began to gain popularity, led by acts such as [Remedies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Remedies), Trybes Men, JJC, etc. Throughout the years, [highlife music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highlife) retained its popularity in the country.

At the turn of the century, famous 2000s acts like [P-Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P-Square), [2face](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2Baba), and [Dbanj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D'banj) were credited to have made tremendous impact in the evolution of [Afrobeats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrobeats) and its popularisation on the international stage.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388]

In November 2008, Nigeria's music scene (and that of Africa) received international attention when MTV hosted the continent's first African music awards show in Abuja.[^cite_note-389] Over a decade later, the Afrobeat genre has widely taken over, with artist like [Davido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davido), [Wizkid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wizkid) and [Burna Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burna_Boy).

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Nigeria)

> Top five [highest grossing Nigerian films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_Nigerian_films) as at 2024:
>
> - [Everybody Loves Jenifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everybody_Loves_Jenifa) (₦1.7 billion) - 2024 film
>
> - [A Tribe Called Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Tribe_Called_Judah) (₦1.4 billion) - 2023 film
>
> - [Battle on Buka Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_on_Buka_Street) (₦668 million) - 2022 film
>
> - *[Omo Ghetto: The Saga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omo_Ghetto:_The_Saga)* (₦636 million) - 2020 film
>
>   [^cite_note-Top_20_films_9th_15th_April_2021_-_Cinema_Exhibitors_Association_of_Nigeria-390]
>
> - [Alakada: Bad and Boujee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alakada:_Bad_and_Boujee) (₦460 million) - 2024 film

The Nigerian film industry is known as [Nollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nollywood) (a [blend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blend_word) of "Nigeria" and "Hollywood")[^cite_note-391] and is now the second-largest producer of movies in the world, having surpassed Hollywood. Only India's [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood) is larger. Nigerian [film studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_studios) are based in [Lagos](85232.md), [Kano](508782.md), and [Enugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enugu_\(city\)), and form a major portion of the local economy of these cities. Nigerian cinema is [Africa](5334607.md)'s largest movie industry in terms of both value and the number of movies produced per year. Although Nigerian films have been produced since the 1960s, the country's film industry has been aided by the rise of affordable [digital filming and editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_cinema) technologies. The 2009 thriller film *[The Figurine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Figurine)* heightened the media attention towards the [New Nigerian Cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Nigerian_Cinema) revolution. The film was a critical and commercial success in Nigeria, and it was also screened in international film festivals.[^cite_note-Thorburn,_Jane2-392] The 2010 film *[Ijé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ij%C3%A9)* by Chineze Anyaene, overtook *The Figurine* to become the [highest-grossing Nigerian film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest-grossing_films_in_Nigeria); a record it held for four years until it was overtaken in 2014 by *[Half of a Yellow Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half_of_a_Yellow_Sun_\(film\))* (2013).[^cite_note-The_Economist2-393][^cite_note-394] By 2016, this record was held by *[The Wedding Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wedding_Party_\(2016_film\))* by [Kemi Adetiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemi_Adetiba). ‘[My Father’s Shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Father's_Shadow)’ by [Akinola Davies Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akinola_Davies_Jr.) won awards at [Cannes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Cannes_Film_Festival) and the [BAFTAs](150200.md) in 2025.[^cite_note-395]

By the end of 2013, the film industry reportedly hit a record-breaking revenue of ₦1.72 trillion (US\$4.1 billion). As of 2014, the industry was worth ₦853.9 billion (US\$5.1 billion), making it the third most valuable film industry in the world behind the [United States](3434750.md) and [India](14533.md). It contributed about 1.4% to Nigeria's economy; this was attributed to the increase in the number of quality films produced and more formal distribution methods.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

[T.B. Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T.B._Joshua)'s [Emmanuel TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_TV), originating from Nigeria, is one of the most viewed television stations across Africa.[^cite_note-398]

### Festival

Main article: [Festivals in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festivals_in_Nigeria)



Ofala Festival of Onitsha People

There are many festivals in Nigeria, some of which date to the period before the arrival of the major religions in this ethnically and culturally diverse society. The main Muslim and Christian festivals are often celebrated in ways that are unique to Nigeria or unique to the people of a locality.[^cite_note-OnlineNigeria2-399] The Nigerian Tourism Development Corporation has been working with the states to upgrade the traditional festivals, which may become important sources of tourism revenue.[^cite_note-Oxford20102-400]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Nigerian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_cuisine)

Jollof rice with fried fish and plantain, garnished with cucumber and tomatoes

Jollof rice with fried fish and plantain, garnished with cucumber and tomatoes

Nigerian cuisine, like West African cuisine in general, is known for its richness and variety. Many different spices, herbs, and flavourings are used in conjunction with [palm oil](57561.md) or [groundnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peanut) oil to create deeply flavoured sauces and soups often made very hot with [chilli peppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_pepper). Nigerian feasts are colourful and lavish, while aromatic market and roadside snacks cooked on barbecues or fried in oil are plentiful and varied. [Suya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suya) is usually sold in urban areas especially during night-time.[^cite_note-401]

### Fashion

Main article: [Fashion in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_in_Nigeria)

Àdìrẹ, colourful Nigerian tie-dyed fabric

[Àdìrẹ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adire_\(textile_art\))

The fashion industry in Nigeria contributes significantly to the country's economics. Casual attire is commonly worn but formal and traditional styles are also worn depending on the occasion. Nigeria is known not only for its fashionable textiles and garments, but also for its fashion designers who have increasingly gained international recognition.
